 # Fall 2026 Plan
-==Accomplish what is written down, write down only what needs to be accomplished==. A detailed planned source of truth for the fall semester plan.
+==Accomplish what is written down, write down only what needs to be accomplished.== The frame this plan runs on - one goal, everything else scoped or cut against it - is [[10_Areas/Life/Plans/Fall 2026/Fall 2026 - The One Thing|Fall 2026 - The One Thing]]. Read that first; this note is the checkable system underneath it.
 - Full process for using this template: [[10_Areas/Life/Plans/Plans-to-Create|Plans-to-Create]]. Cadence and staleness rules: [[30_Order/Standards/Daily Workflow Standard|Daily Workflow Standard]].
+
 ## Goal
-- 21 Credits are laid out which need to be completed with a 4.0 GPA. Stick to what you say, make it a reality.
-- TradingView needs to be running and printing money by september end.
-- Internship applications flooding to companies, the loop is completely rigid and exactly setup. 
-- Arc is built by the end of the semester.
-- Jarvis is a real second brain with everything working and in use.
-- The Plan sync is active and working. The daily and self improvement is in place, working perfectly.
-- AIIS Club is on point and putting all of my work over there.
-- Find fellowships or some paid work that promotes my resume for fall 2026. Do the work correctly and accurately.
-- UROP for jaideep srivastava officially and working perfectly.
-- Utilizing the new laptop alongside the setup configured.
-- Network with over 100 people - meetings.
-- Attend at least 5 conferences and 5 hackathons this september.
-- Mentorship program for 2 mentors on plan and working for my use - laid out and completely responsive. 
-- Apply for scholarships and find money schemes in u.s.
-- 
+Land a real Summer 2027 software engineering or AI/ML internship offer, proven by moving off zero applications sent (0 in `10_Areas/Career/Internships/Tracker/Each One/Applied/` as of 2026-09-07, against 18 already researched in `.../Current/`) before this plan's 2026-12-14 deadline.
+
+Non-negotiable regardless of priority:
+- 21 credits across 6 classes, per [[20_Progress/Degree/Fall'26 Syllabus|Fall'26 Syllabus]], at a 4.0. A 7th CSCI class (22 credits total) is pending approval and isn't in that note yet - add it there the day it's confirmed, don't plan hours around a credit count that isn't real.
+- AIIS Club's external deadlines don't move: SAO grant budget and panelist confirmations due 2026-09-10, Kickoff 2026-09-16, joint Fall AI Convention with Nexus on 2026-10-01. Detail in [[40_Resources/UMN/SAO/AIIS|AIIS]].
+
+Recalibrated from the original 2026-08-09 goal list, against real evidence:
+- "TradingView running and printing money by September end" is not achievable on the actual build state: the repo is in Month 1 (data ingestion foundation) per `20_Progress/AI/Claude Code/Trading View/CLAUDE.md`'s own roadmap, and strategy/backtest/AI-analyst work is explicitly gated behind that phase finishing. Recalibrated goal: clear the ingestion foundation's remaining `tasks.md` checkpoints and reach the strategy/backtest phase by semester end. No live-trading or profitability claim belongs in this plan - the repo's own guardrails forbid fabricating results.
+- "Mentorship program for 2 mentors, laid out and completely responsive" - Ahnaf is real and ongoing; a second mentor is a named live idea, not yet enrolled, per [[20_Progress/Mentorship Program/Plans/Fall 2026 — Detailed Expectations|Fall 2026 - Detailed Expectations]]. Goal here is enrolling the second mentor this fall, not having both "completely responsive" from day one.
+- "Apply for scholarships and find money schemes" - no tracker or shortlist exists anywhere in the vault yet. This is a real gap, tracked as an open system below, not assumed already in motion.
+
+Carried over from the original scope, not yet given a system this session (see [[10_Areas/Life/Plans/Fall 2026/Fall 2026 - The One Thing|Fall 2026 - The One Thing]]#Carried over, not dropped): Arc, the UROP with Jaideep Srivastava, and getting The Plan's sync fully active.

+### Week by week - the One Hard Thing

Every week also carries the permanent floor systems from the Systems table below (application conversion, LeetCode/CodePath, class steps) regardless of which hard thing is picked - the hard thing is the one extra push that week, not a replacement for the floor. Weeks without a real external deadline default to scaling the application floor; weeks with one are named explicitly. Unconfirmed dates (marked below) get corrected the week they're confirmed, not guessed at now.

As of 2026-09-07, the Fall'26 semester runs on an essentialist one-goal frame: land a Summer 2027 SWE/AI-ML internship offer. Two files hold this:
- `10_Areas/Life/Plans/Fall 2026/Fall 2026 - The One Thing.md` — the vision/frame note. States the one goal, splits everything else into "mandatory regardless" (classes, CodePath, system design) vs. "in service of the one thing" (AIIS club, mentorship, scholarships, networking, LinkedIn, Portfolio v2/blog, TradingView, ClaudeKit). Ends with a single `**Action:**` line: move ≥1 company from `Current/` to `Applied/` every day before anything else.
- `10_Areas/Life/Plans/Fall 2026/Fall 2026 Plan.md` — the execution file. Week-by-week "One Hard Thing" table from 2026-09-07 through the 2026-12-14 deadline, a Systems table (cadence + done-definition per track), and an Implementation Status section verified against real files.

**Why:** The user gave a sprawling brain-dump of ~15 concurrent threads (classes, CodePath/LeetCode, system design starting 2026-09-14, AIIS club, mentorship, scholarships, conferences/hackathons/career fair, LinkedIn, portfolio blog/subdomain, TradingView backend, ClaudeKit agentic-OS visualization across two laptops, new laptop setup) and asked for exactly two files: a vision file ending in an `**Action:**` line, and a weekly-milestone/habits/systems file built around one-hard-thing-per-week and an 80/20 daily split run through `/startday`/`/closeday`. See [[feedback_verify_dont_assume_powershell]] for the general verification discipline applied here.

**How to apply:** Before proposing new Fall'26 goals or re-litigating priorities, read these two files first — don't regenerate the plan from scratch. Key baseline numbers as of 2026-09-07 (re-verify before trusting, they move daily): 18 companies researched in `10_Areas/Career/Internships/Tracker/Each One/Current/`, 0 in `Applied/`. `.claude/skills/startday/SKILL.md` and `.claude/skills/weekly-review/weekly-review.md` were repointed from the closed Summer 2026 plan files to these two Fall files, and `00_Dashboard.md`'s daily fields/habit checklist/10_Areas Overview row were refreshed to match — if `/startday` or `/weekly-review` ever reference `10_Areas/Life/Plans/Summer/` again, that's a regression to fix, not a valid read. Known open gaps tracked in the plan's Systems table, not yet built: no scholarship tracker, no LinkedIn content plan, no Fall LeetCode/CodePath daily log, system-design resource not yet chosen, UMN career fair date(s) unconfirmed. `10_Areas/Life/Plans/Plans Board.md` also still lists `Final Month Plan (Jul 28 - Sep 1)` as Active despite its end date having passed — flagged, not closed, since that wasn't in scope for this session.

# _Courses — Non-Degree Study Map
This folder holds every certification and self-study course running alongside the six credit-bearing Fall'26 classes in [[20_Progress/Degree/Fall'26 Syllabus|Fall'26 Syllabus]]. None of these earn UMN credit - they exist because [[10_Areas/Life/Plans/Fall 2026/Fall 2026 - The One Thing|Fall 2026 - The One Thing]] treats technical-interview strength and a real ML/cert portfolio as mandatory regardless of the internship push, not optional extras. As of 2026-09-07 this note is a syllabus and sequencing map, written before most tracks have their own per-course notes - each subfolder below gets its own systematic syllabus the day that course actually starts, per the standing rule the user set this session.

## Map
**GitHub Foundations** is first in the sequence and closest to done. The DataCamp prep course for it is already finished; a GitHub Student Developer Pack promo code is pending, and the exam gets registered and written the day that code arrives. This is the certification base the rest of the cert sequence sits on top of.

**AI Associate Engineer** starts right after GitHub Foundations closes out. Its 30-day exam window is already running with 2 attempts allowed - the real deadline is finishing the underlying course and sitting the exam by **2026-09-30**, not a soft target.

**ML Engineer** (DataCamp) and **ML Ops** (a DataTalks.club repo, almost certainly the MLOps Zoomcamp) run in parallel with the above, on no fixed end date. Both are "going on for a while" by design - the deliverable isn't the course completion certificate, it's implementing what each course teaches in a real local repo. Which repo that is hasn't been decided yet; naming it is an open task, not a gap to paper over.

**Technical Interview** prep starts now, while GitHub Foundations and AI Associate Engineer are still running - it doesn't wait for either to finish. Cadence: LeetCode every day, plus CodePath/CSCI 4041's course-provided material every Tuesday and Thursday. CSCI 4041 itself is not one of the six credit-bearing Fall'26 classes in [[20_Progress/Degree/Fall'26 Syllabus|Fall'26 Syllabus]] - it's a helper track that exists to force the LeetCode reps this folder tracks, confirmed directly by the user on 2026-09-07.

**System Design** has no fixed start date. It begins once GitHub Foundations, AI Associate Engineer, and the LeetCode/CodePath floor all reach real stability, then runs gradually and self-paced from there via GitHub-sourced courses and links not yet chosen. This supersedes the original [[10_Areas/Life/Plans/Fall 2026/Fall 2026 Plan|Fall 2026 Plan]] line that named 2026-09-14 as a hard start date - that date is wrong as of this session and has been corrected in that plan.

**Cloud Architect** (LinkedIn Learning) is the newest addition to this folder, and the only track with a hard future start date: **2026-11-01**, when a one-month LinkedIn Learning / Premium free trial begins. The weeks between now and then are not idle - they're for building and perfecting the LinkedIn outreach and posting process itself (templates, cadence, what actually gets a response) before spending the free-trial month on it. On 2026-11-01 both things ride the same subscription window: the Cloud Architect course gets taken through LinkedIn Learning, and outreach runs at maximum volume using whatever the trial unlocks (InMail, expanded search) - not a "someday" goal, a one-month spike timed to the trial.

CSCI 2033 lives in the Degree folder alongside these but isn't a Fall'26 class either - it's linear-algebra review material serving as helper prep for CSCI 5304 (Matrix Theory), per the same 2026-09-07 clarification that placed CSCI 4041 here.

## Status
| Track | State as of 2026-09-07 | Target |
|---|---|---|
| GitHub Foundations | DataCamp prep finished; promo code pending | Exam written as soon as the code arrives |
| AI Associate Engineer | 30-day exam window already running, 2 attempts | Course finished and exam passed by 2026-09-30 |
| ML Engineer (DataCamp) | Ongoing, parallel, no end date set | Implemented in a real local repo - repo not yet named |
| ML Ops (DataTalks) | Ongoing, parallel, no end date set | Implemented in a real local repo - same open item |
| Technical Interview | Starting now | LeetCode daily; CSCI 4041/CodePath material Tue + Thu |
| System Design | Not started | Begins once the three tracks above are stable - no date, resource not chosen |
| Cloud Architect (LinkedIn) | Not started | Begins 2026-11-01 with the LinkedIn Learning/Premium free trial |

## Sequence
1. Now: ML Engineer + ML Ops run in the background; GitHub Foundations closes out; Technical Interview prep starts.
2. GitHub Foundations exam written → AI Associate Engineer's 30-day clock is the active deadline, due 2026-09-30.
3. Once GitHub Foundations, AI Associate Engineer, and the LeetCode/CodePath floor are all stable → System Design starts, self-paced.
4. Now through 2026-10-31: refine the LinkedIn outreach/posting process - no certification work here yet.
5. 2026-11-01: LinkedIn Learning/Premium free trial starts - Cloud Architect course + maximum outreach push, same month.

**Technical Interview** prep starts now, while GitHub Foundations and AI Associate Engineer are still running - it doesn't wait for either to finish. # Fall 2026 Builds
This is the build-project counterpart to [[20_Progress/Degree/_Courses/_Courses Board|_Courses Board]] - that note maps what gets studied this fall, this one maps what gets built. Everything here answers to the same frame in [[10_Areas/Life/Plans/Fall 2026/Fall 2026 - The One Thing|Fall 2026 - The One Thing]]: none of these get more hours than the internship push, and each one exists either because it's already real and worth maintaining, or because it produces a concrete interview story. Written 2026-09-07 against real files, not memory - where a claim can't be verified from inside Jarvis, that's stated outright instead of rounded up.

## Map

**TradingView** already has real guardrails against fabricated data and execution language (`20_Progress/AI/Claude Code/Trading View/CLAUDE.md`), but the `Trading View/` folder in this vault is only a synced mirror of instructions - README, CLAUDE.md, AGENTS.md, Setup.md, and a Sync-Log that just records periodic file-copy timestamps. There's no `src/`, no `.kiro/`, no actual code here, so its real build state can't be verified from Jarvis. Its own two docs disagree: CLAUDE.md (edited 2026-08-10) says the current phase is "Month 1 - Data Ingestion Foundation" with strategy work explicitly gated behind it; README.md (edited 2026-08-21, eleven days more recent) claims a "year-ahead base" is already complete on `main` - ingestion, a brain closed loop, factor math, fundamentals (FMP/SEC), a four-gate promotion harness, and paper-test contracts. Neither claim is confirmed from here. The Fall goal, regardless of which status is current: a basic UI to look at and test features, a self-improving ingestion system, and real notes on the specific market niche worked into an actual strategy - matching the repo's own Roadmap (`live-data shakeout → RankIC/Kronos validation → agents on evidence packets → UI/charts`). Every guardrail in CLAUDE.md still applies to whatever gets written this fall, including strategy notes: no execution language, no fabricated data, confidence always capped by real data quality.

**Portfolio** is a mature, active build - Next.js 16, Sanity CMS, Clerk auth, an Orby chatbot with an eval harness, security hardening phases 1 through 5, an SEO/AEO strategy, and a Blog section already implemented per `20_Progress/Projects/CS/Portfolio/frontend/Ran/08 - Blog, Contact & Footer.md`. What's new as of 2026-09-07: v2 ships first (the current build), but real blog hosting and posting ramps up in what the user calls "v3" - a term that doesn't exist anywhere else in the vault yet, just stated intent, not a scoped iteration. Once v3 deploys: publish weekly, share resources, post about each one on LinkedIn, and (a "maybe," not a commitment) explore organic AI-generated ad marketing across social platforms. Two real monetization goals, both currently unscoped: ads/sponsors for individual posts, and some form of income generation on the subdomain itself. None of the mechanisms for either are chosen yet.

**ClaudeKit / the agentic OS visualization** carries over unchanged from the original scope: `second-brain-claudekit` already runs a real qualification pipeline (sandbox → tested-tools → promotion) and syncs Windows ↔ WSL via Unison, with a known, unresolved permission-bit failure on the Windows side visible in its own `Sync-Log.md`. This is maintenance on infrastructure the internship-research-loop's 24/7 discovery automation already depends on, not a new build.

**Jarvis daily use, alongside The Plan's sync** also carries over unchanged - Jarvis gets used daily as the actual second brain it's built to be, and getting The Plan's sync fully active is still an open item with no system built for it yet.

**Arc (Learning Tracker) is dropped**, reversing its "carried over, not dropped" status from the original scope. Its planning docs live at `20_Progress/Projects/CS/Arc/` - a 5-sprint roadmap and a data-model sketch, no code ever written, per the Summer close-out's own verdict ("Planning docs exist but don't count as a real scope - no code"). They're slated for deletion but that hasn't happened yet - nothing in this session deleted them.

**Jarvis automations** is the one genuinely new, unscoped build this session: a weekend (no date set yet) spent building real automations across Gmail, Google Calendar, and other information-providing tools, plus a pipeline that ingests videos shared from the phone - Reels, YouTube videos watched - landing in Obsidian somehow. `20_Progress/Projects/Automations/` already exists as a folder but is completely empty - nothing built, nothing scoped. Two vault notes are worth checking before choosing a tool stack: `10_Areas/Career/Internships/Contacts/Outreach Discovery & Automation Status.md` already ruled n8n out for LinkedIn specifically (login-walled, actively detected) - whether that verdict extends to Gmail/GCal automation is a real open question, not assumed; and `40_Resources/Obsidian/Plugins/AI Automation and Local Interfaces.md` may already cover relevant local-automation options worth reading first instead of starting from zero.

**Hackathons** - the user referenced "other hackathons coming up, enrolled for" but no specific hackathon, date, or enrollment record exists anywhere in the vault as of this session. `10_Areas/Career/Hackathon/Hackathons.md` holds the judge-credibility playbook and prep checklist, not a roster of what's actually signed up for. This is a real gap: name the specific hackathons once they're confirmed, don't assume the playbook alone covers it.

## Evidence Discipline
The user asked directly for "systematic proof notes" going forward - every claim above gets checked against a real file, a real commit, or an explicit "unverified" flag, the same Implementation-Status convention [[10_Areas/Life/Plans/Fall 2026/Fall 2026 Plan|Fall 2026 Plan]] already runs on. This note follows that convention rather than inventing a separate proof-note format. Per the user's own instruction, the next step after this note is a deeper information-gathering pass across everything discussed this session, then - on a future prompt, not this one - sub-notes get written for exactly how each build actually gets done. Nothing beyond this map gets built or written until that prompt.

# Cloud Architect
==This is the one track in [[20_Progress/Degree/_Courses/_Courses Board|_Courses Board]] with a hard future start date and no chosen curriculum yet - 2026-11-01, when a one-month LinkedIn Learning/Premium free trial begins.== Everything below is scope, not a syllabus, because there's nothing to be specific about until the actual course is picked.

## What This Is (So Far)
A Cloud Architect certification path, taken through LinkedIn Learning during a one-month free trial. Which specific LinkedIn Learning course, learning path, or vendor track (AWS/Azure/GCP Solutions Architect are the likely candidates, per how LinkedIn Learning typically packages this) hasn't been chosen - naming it is the open task before 2026-11-01, not something to guess at here. Nothing about this track should be treated as decided beyond the start date and the delivery platform.

## Why This One
Cloud architecture certifications signal directly to the fullstack/backend and infrastructure-adjacent roles in the internship tracker (`10_Areas/Career/Internships/Tracker/`) - a real, checkable credential beats a resume line with no proof behind it. Whether it's worth the hours against [[10_Areas/Life/Plans/Fall 2026/Fall 2026 - The One Thing|Fall 2026 - The One Thing]] depends entirely on which specific course gets chosen and how much real hands-on build work it requires versus passive video-watching - that judgment can't be made honestly until the course is named.

## Why 2026-11-01 and Not Sooner
The weeks between now and 2026-11-01 aren't idle time for this track - they're for building and perfecting the LinkedIn outreach and posting process itself (templates, cadence, what gets a response), separate from this course entirely. Bundling the Cloud Architect course into the same one-month window as a maximized outreach push is deliberate: both ride the same LinkedIn Learning/Premium subscription, so the trial month gets used for outreach volume (InMail, expanded search) and structured learning at the same time, instead of paying for the trial twice at different times.

## Open Tasks Before 2026-11-01
- Pick the actual LinkedIn Learning course or learning path - not done yet.
- Confirm what the "LinkedIn Learning / Premium free trial" actually includes (LinkedIn Learning and LinkedIn Premium Career are sometimes bundled, sometimes separate trials) - unverified from inside this vault, worth checking directly on LinkedIn before 2026-11-01 rather than assuming.
- Have the outreach/posting process (templates, cadence) already working *before* 2026-11-01, so the trial month is spent using it at volume, not building it from scratch.

## Resources
None chosen yet. This section gets filled in once the specific course is picked - do not add a placeholder link here to make the note look more complete than it is.

Full sequencing (GitHub Foundations → AI Associate Engineer → System Design → Cloud Architect, with ML Engineer/ML Ops running parallel throughout) lives in [[20_Progress/Degree/_Courses/_Courses Board|_Courses Board]]. # Autonomous loop check

You're being invoked on a timer while the user is away or occupied. The point is to keep work moving forward without the user driving every step - finishing things they started, maintaining PRs they're building, catching problems before they come back to find them. You're a steward, not an initiator. The user set you loose on their work, and the value you provide comes from reliably advancing things they've already set in motion, not from finding new things to do.

The key tension to navigate: the user trusts you enough to run autonomously, but that trust is easily lost. Acting on what the conversation already established is safe and valuable. Inventing new work or making irreversible changes without clear authorization erodes trust fast. When you're unsure whether something falls into "continuing established work" or "inventing new work," lean toward the former only when the transcript provides clear evidence the user wanted it done. If you find yourself reaching for justifications about why a push is probably fine, that's a signal to wait.

## What to act on

The current conversation is your highest-signal source - re-read the transcript above, since everything there is something the user was actively engaged with. The strongest signal is an in-progress PR you've been building together: review comments to address and resolve, failing CI checks to diagnose (and re-enqueue if they're flakes), merge conflicts to fix. The goal is to get the PR into a state where it's ready to merge pending only human review - the user shouldn't come back to find a PR blocked on things you could have handled. After that, look for unfinished implementation where the last exchange left something half-done, and explicit "I'll also..." or "next I'll..." commitments the conversation made and didn't honor. Weaker but still real: dangling questions you could now answer, verification steps that were skipped, edge cases that were mentioned but not handled, and natural continuations that don't require new decisions.

If you find anything in this category, act on it - actually do the work, don't describe what could be done. Run the tests, don't say "you could run the tests." The whole point of autonomous operation is that work gets done while the user is away.

When the conversation transcript has nothing left, the current branch's pull/merge request on the user's SCM is the next-best place to look. This is maintenance work - valuable, but lower priority than continuing the user's active work. Find the PR/MR for the current branch via the SCM's CLI, then check three things: CI status, unresolved review threads, and whether the branch has fallen behind the base. For failing CI, pull the failing job's logs and diagnose before acting - flaky-shaped failures (timeout, runner died, transient network) can be re-enqueued; real failures need a reproduction and a minimal fix. For unresolved review threads, fetch the comment, address the feedback, push, and resolve the thread via, for example, the GitHub GraphQL `resolveReviewThread` mutation (or the equivalent for whichever SCM the project uses). Before pushing anything, check whether someone else has pushed to the branch while you were working - if so, rebase (don't merge) to keep history clean.

When CI is green, threads are clear, and there's idle time, sweeping the branch for issues is a good use of that time - bug-hunt or simplification passes catch problems before reviewers do, saving everyone a round-trip.

If everything is genuinely quiet - no conversation work, no PR maintenance - say so in one sentence and stop. No summary of what you checked, no list of what you might do later. The user will see your message in the transcript when they come back; three consecutive "nothing to do" results means you should scale back to a quick CI check and stop, not narrate.

## Repeated invocations

If you see earlier autonomous checks in this conversation, adjust your scope accordingly. If a previous check left a question the user hasn't answered, the cost of acting depends on reversibility: for reversible actions (local edits, running tests), make your best call and proceed; for irreversible ones (pushing, deleting, sending), keep waiting - the cost of acting wrongly on something irreversible is much higher than the cost of waiting one more cycle. If three or more consecutive checks have found nothing actionable, things are quiet - do one quick CI/threads check and stop in a single line. Repeated "nothing to do" messages clutter the transcript and waste the user's attention when they come back to review.

Read and analyze freely - understanding the state of things has no blast radius. Make edits and run tests when you're confident they continue established work. Commit and push only when you're clearly continuing something the user authorized, or when the work pattern makes the intent obvious - like fixing CI on a PR you've been building together.

Course Syllabus
Welcome to CSCI 4061! This course will serve as an introduction to modern computer systems and their support for the many different applications of computing we see today. The beginning of the course will discuss systems programming and the effective use of operating systems primitives, with Linux as the primary example. We will then continue on to newer topics such as distributed systems.

We will start looking at these topics through the lens of systems programming in C and then progress to programming in Python. We’ll also study and implement software that involves interoperation between code written in both languages.

Understanding how computer systems work will enable you to use these systems to develop more sophisticated and more efficient software applications, no matter which language or set of tools you use in the future. This course will also prepare you for further study in courses on operating systems, compilers, databases, and networking.

Course Catalog Description
Processes/threads, process coordination, interprocess communication, asynchronous events, memory management, file systems. Systems programming projects using operating system interfaces and program development tools.

Prerequisites
Grade of C- or Better in CSCI 2021 or EE 2361
Admission to Computer Science Major or Minor, Computer Engineering Major
In addition to the formal prerequisites, a solid understanding of C programming is essential to completing this course. If you are out of practice with C, you will need to thoroughly review this material. We have a recommended list of resources for you to consult.

Learning Objectives
Upon completion of CSCI 4061, students will be able to:

Understand some of the central abstractions that most operating systems provide. This includes processes, files, threads, synchronization, and communication both within a single machine and over a network.
Explain why these abstractions allow an operating system to provide security, integrity, flexibility, and efficiency.
Understand the benefits and drawbacks of parallelism and concurrency and write programs involving multiple processes and threads.
Understand and reason about distributed systems, their key ideas (such as replication, partitioning, consistency, and consensus), and related network protocols.
Productively engage in systems programming, involving various OS and networking primitives, at a low level of abstraction with C and at a higher level of abstraction with Python.
Course Textbooks
Advanced Programming in the UNIX Environment, Third Edition, by W. Richard Stevens and Stephen A. Rago, 2013. (Required)
Designing Data-Intensive Applications, First Edition, by Martin Kleppmann, 2017. (Required)
Computing Environment
As we will be studying Unix-based operating systems (Linux in particular) and distributed systems, you will need to work within a standardized Linux environment to complete the assignments in this class. Each student will set up Docker on their personal machine to provide such an environment. We will assist you with this setup in our first laboratory activity. We will then grade all of your submitted code based on its execution and behavior in this environment.

It is your responsibility to set up and maintain a working environment in which to complete the work for this class. Difficulties with your environment are not sufficient grounds for deadline extensions. Make sure you back up your work regularly, as data loss is also not grounds for assignment extensions.

Information and advice on environment setup has been collected in our Programming Environment Guide.

Thursday, November 12: Midterm Exam 2, In Class, 75 Minutes

Course Tools
Canvas: The CSCI 4061 Canvas website will be the main source of information for this class. We will post all important information such as the office hours schedule, course assignments, and grades here. It is your responsibility to check this site regularly to stay up to date on course information.
Piazza: We will use Piazza as our class Q&A forum. You are encouraged to post questions here about lecture material, course assignments, etc. Please be mindful of the university’s Policy for online learning. Respect and civility towards others is expected at all times on Piazza.
GradescopeLinks to an external site.: We will use Gradescope to handle submissions of programming assignments for grading. We will also use Gradescope to grade exams and to handle exam regrade requests.
Course Assignments and Grading
Grade Components
Final grades will be computed from the following components:

Weekly Canvas Quizzes: 10% (Lowest Two Scores Dropped)
Laboratory Assignments: 10% (Lowest Two Scores Dropped)
Course Projects: 25%
Midterm Exam 1: 15%
Midterm Exam 2: 15%
Final Exam (Cumulative): 20%
Surveys: 5%
Grading Scale
Final grades will be determined by the following criteria without rounding.

Percentage    Letter Grade
93 and Above    A
90-92    A-
87-89    B+
83-86    B
80-82    B-
77-79    C+
73-76    C
70-72    C-
65-69    D+
60-64    D
59 and Below    F
If circumstances require it, the grading scale will be adjusted in the students’ favor with a curve determined at the conclusion of the course. That is, for a given percentage, grades may be higher than what is listed here but will not be lower.

Laboratory Assignments
Lectures serve to introduce new material, while laboratory sessions are intended to solidify your understanding and cover additional material that will prove useful in the course projects. They are also a good way to meet your classmates and to find project teammates.

Regularly attending lab sessions is an important part of succeeding in this course. Therefore, attendance at each week’s lab session will be required to receive credit for the week’s assignment. You are welcome and encouraged to collaborate with other students on lab assignments.

Lab assignments will be submitted on Gradescope and are due at 11:59pm on the Wednesday following each lab session. Late work is not accepted for lab assignments. We will drop each student’s two lowest lab scores when computing final grades — this is intended to address situations in which you need to miss a lab meeting.

Canvas Quizzes
Each week, a quiz will be posted on the course Canvas website. Quiz questions will ask you to apply material from recent lectures, labs, and reading assignments.

These quizzes are graded automatically and permit ten attempts. Quizzes will be due on Monday of each week at 11:59pm. As with labs, late work is not accepted for weekly quizzes. We will drop each student’s two lowest quiz scores when computing final grades.

Weekly quizzes are open resource and open for collaboration with other students in the course. However, your answers must reflect your own understanding of the material, in accordance with the academic integrity policy described below.

Projects
Much of the learning in this class takes place through programming projects that will ask you to apply and expand upon the material presented in lecture, discussions, and labs. Each project will involve writing programs and an oral exam to demonstrate understanding of your submission’s design and functionality. Pay careful attention to the guidelines presented in each project’s specification. Projects are usually large and will require a significant amount of work to complete.

Projects may be completed individually or in teams of two people, but oral exams will be individual. Teams of three or more people are not allowed. You are not allowed to discuss project details outside of your team. Each individual or team’s submission must reflect their own work and understanding of the material, in accordance with the academic integrity policy presented below.

If your partnership is dissolved partway through the project, you must complete the rest of the project individually. Finding a new partner is not permitted at this point. You are welcome to find a new partner at the beginning of the next project.

Project solutions will be submitted through Gradescope, which will usually then run the submitted code against a set of test cases to check for correct behavior. We will usually make a subset of these tests available to students. You are free to submit your code to Gradescope as long as the submission window is open. However, we may limit rapid resubmission to ensure the stability of the grading system. Note that your project grade is determined by your latest submission.

It is your responsibility to verify that any results produced by Gradescope match your expectations. You should not assume that the results of any tests you run locally will be perfectly replicated by the autograder. Due to the large size of the class, we will not be able to manually repair or regrade your code after the deadline has passed.

We will also manually inspect your code and assign points based on the extent to which your program properly handles error conditions. This is an essential aspect of systems programming and of developing robust software in general.

Project grades are determined by three broad components:

Results of test cases, either run automatically by Gradescope on submission or run by course staff against all submissions after the project deadline.
An individual oral exam with a member of the course staff in which you will be asked questions about your submission’s design and functionality. You will be graded based on your ability to explain and analyze your work.
Manual inspection of your code by course staff to evaluate the extent to which your code properly handles error conditions.
Late Project Submissions
Late project submissions are accepted according to the following rules:

Submissions up to the deadline receive no penalty.
Submissions up to 24 hours beyond the deadline are assigned 90% of the credit they would have received if submitted on time.
Submissions up to 48 hours beyond the deadline are assigned 80% of the credit they would have received if submitted on time.
Submissions received more than 48 hours beyond the deadline receive no credit.
For example, a project solution that receives 80 points if submitted on time is assigned 72 points if submitted up to 24 hours late, 64 points if submitted up to 48 hours late, and 0 points if submitted any later.

Exams
We will have two midterm exams and one final exam. The two midterms are 75 minutes in length and will be administered during our normal lecture meeting time. The cumulative final exam will be administered at the time assigned by the university and will be 120 minutes in length.

Exams will be graded using Gradescope. We will also use Gradescope to enable regrade requests. If you believe an error was made in the grading of a particular exam question, you may request that it be regraded. Be specific and respectful in your requests – we only assign credit based on what is written on the exam itself and not based on any post-hoc explanations. Requests submitted after the end of the designated regrade period will not be considered.

Collaboration with others, in any form, is prohibited on exams. You should assume as a default that the use of any resources (electronics, note sheets, textbooks, etc.), is prohibited for all exams. We may decide to allow students to consult a fixed number of letter-size sheets of paper for use as a reference during exams, but reserve the right to prohibit the use of any resources during exams. The resource policy for each exam will be determined and communicated to students well before the exam to give everyone adequate notice.

Any violation of these rules will be treated as a violation of the course academic integrity policy detailed below.

Extra Credit
There are no extra credit opportunities in this course.

Extensions and Make-Up Work
Students will not be penalized for absence during the semester due to unavoidable or legitimate circumstances. We will adhere strictly to the university’s policy on excused absences and makeup work to determine when accommodations are warranted and what the nature of these accommodations will be.

Withdrawals and Incompletes
You are permitted to withdraw from this class, without special approval, until the end of Week 4. See the University Calendar for exact dates. After that time, approval to withdraw may be granted by the college administration only in extraordinary circumstances.

Incompletes will be given only on request, and then only at the discretion of the instructor if there are extraordinary circumstances warranting it and you have already completed a substantial portion of the work for this class in a timely fashion.

Office Hours
The office hours schedule will be posted on the main page of our Canvas website. All office hours are open to students enrolled in any of the sections of 4061 governed by this syllabus.

No appointment is necessary, but usually students receive help on a first-come first-serve basis at office hours. If office hours become especially busy, course staff may enforce a time limit of ten minutes per student to ensure their attention is allocated equitably to as many students as possible.

Academic Integrity
All work you submit (e.g., assignment answers, project code, exam answers) must be a result of your own effort and reflect your individual understanding of the material (or, for projects, your team’s effort and understanding). You may be asked at any time to explain your work. Failure to do so will be construed as evidence of academic dishonesty.

Examples of academic dishonesty include, but are not limited to, the following:

Directly copying someone else’s solution to a problem, including student solutions from a previous semester
Directly copying an answer from some outside source such as the Internet or a friend
Making use of an instructor solution manual to complete problems
Using Generative AI tools to produce all or part of your solution (more information below)
Submitting someone else’s work as your own
Paying someone for solutions to assignments or exams
Posting solutions to any website including public posts to our course sites
Collaborating or copying the work of others during an exam
Taking another student’s code with or without their consent
Giving another student one’s own code on assignments or exams
Aiding or abetting any of the above
Witnessing any of the above and failing to report it to an instructor immediately
Additionally, note that these rules apply at all times, including in office hours. Being present at an office hour session does not make it acceptable for you to discuss or share solution details with other students.

The following links offer more information about University and Department academic integrity policies:

University Student Conduct Code
Computer Science Department Policies on Scholastic Dishonesty
Office of Community Standards
Use of Generative Artificial Intelligence Tools
Generative AI tools are clearly transforming the way software is produced. However, these tools cannot be used effectively or responsibly without an understanding of the underlying fundamentals of computation and sound engineering practices, much of which we cover in this course. AI tools are not substitutes for the intellectual effort, critical thinking, and judgment that you are expected to engage in during this course and that you will need to succeed in whatever endeavors you choose to pursue long after this class is over.

Therefore, only specific and limited uses of AI tools are permitted in this class. You are welcome to use AI to:

Learn about course concepts (example prompt: “What are the tradeoffs between processes and threads for parallel programming?”)
Investigate an error message or bug you are encountering in your code: (example prompt: “Why does Python raise a ValueError when …?”)
Generate examples of how to implement a general concept relevant to this class (example prompt: “Show me an example of a C program that ignores the SIGINT signal type.”)
You are not allowed to use AI to generate all or part of the code that you submit as your work for labs or projects. Working through this code for yourself is the best way to ensure you are learning and building your skills. Examples of improper use of AI include (but are not limited to):

Providing an AI tool with a project specification and tests and asking it to generate a solution for you.
Asking an AI tool to implement a specific function within your project submission for you
Asking an AI tool to fix a bug or error that you observe when working on your program. You are welcome to use AI tools to help you understand the cause of the issue, but you should write the fix yourself.
If you are unsure whether a particular use of AI is allowed, assume it is not and consult the instructor before proceeding.

As the AI landscape continues to evolve, this policy is subject to revision during the semester. We will communicate any changes via Piazza and in lecture.

Penalties
Any instance of misconduct that is detected will be referred to the Office of Community Standards and will likely result in failing the course. Be advised that we will be employing advanced software to detect plagiarism and that this detection is generally robust to attempts at obfuscation.

Workload Statement
In accordance with University Policy, you should expect to devote a weekly average of approximately three hours to this course for each course credit earned. This is a four-credit course; therefore expect about 12 hours of work per week for this class.

Mental Health
As a student you may experience a range of issues that can cause barriers to learning, such as strained relationships, increased anxiety, alcohol/drug problems, feeling down, difficulty concentrating and/or lack of motivation. These mental health concerns or stressful events may lead to diminished academic performance and may reduce your ability to participate in daily activities. University services are available to assist you. You can learn more about the broad range of confidential mental health services available on campus via the Student Mental Health Website. As a course staff, we care about the wellbeing of students. If health, safety, or mental health concerns are conveyed, we may consult with campus support offices to provide support and resources to a student.

Disability Services
The University of Minnesota is committed to creating learning environments that are inclusive and accessible to all students. If you are experiencing disability-related barriers to learning in your courses, the Disability Resource Center (DRC) is the office that collaborates with students to explore reasonable accommodations, tools, and resources.

If you are registered with the DRC and have a current accommodation letter, please share your letter with the instructor as soon as possible so that we can discuss how your accommodations will be implemented in this course. The sooner we know about your disability access-needs, the more equipped we can be to facilitate accommodations. You may reach out to the instructor or your (access consultant/disability specialist) if you have any questions or concerns about your accommodations.
If you are not registered with the DRC and are experiencing or think you may be experiencing disability related to a mental health, attention, learning, chronic health, sensory, or physical condition, and would like to discuss accommodations and/or resources, please contact the DRC on our campus.
Students with DRC accommodations are subject to additional course policies.

General Policies

# CSCI 4061 — Introduction to Operating Systems
Fall'26, 4 credits, in person. Closes the Introduction to Operating Systems sub-requirement of the Computer Science Core — the last open CS Core sub-req per [[APAS]], so this class finishes CS Core outright. Real syllabus pasted in full by the user 2026-09-08, from the course's Canvas page.
## Source of truth
`D:\Users\_Anant\10_Areas\UMN\Classes\CSCI\CSCI 4061` remains the real folder for slides, PDFs, and any future Canvas exports — this note is the readable distillation, not a replacement for the primary files. Desktop shortcut: `CSCI 4061.lnk` in `D:\Users\_Anant\Inbox\`.
## What This Course Actually Covers
An introduction to modern computer systems and how operating systems support them. Starts with systems programming and OS primitives on Linux, moves into distributed systems. Taught first through C, then Python, then interop between the two — understanding both levels is the point, not just passing tests. Course catalog description: processes/threads, process coordination, interprocess communication, asynchronous events, memory management, file systems, systems programming projects using OS interfaces and dev tools.
## Instructor & TAs
Primary instructor: **Jack Kolb** (jhkolb@umn.edu, 300F Lind Hall).
| TA | Email | Role |
|---|---|---|
| Guangyan Sun | sun01158@umn.edu | 50% Grad TA |
| Pengju Liu | liu03486@umn.edu | 50% Grad TA |
| Zhipeng Lu | lu000728@umn.edu | 50% Grad TA |
| Akul Mundada | munda057@umn.edu | Ugrad TA |
| Ben Mechels | meche046@umn.edu | Ugrad TA |
| Jason Jiang | jian0905@umn.edu | Ugrad TA |
| Ken Vang | vang3832@umn.edu | Ugrad TA |
| Krivan Semlani | semla014@umn.edu | Ugrad TA |
## Meetings
Two sections run under this syllabus - confirm which one applies before trusting any date below.
| Section | Lecture | Lab |
|---|---|---|
| 001 | TuTh 1:00–2:15pm, 101 Tate Hall | Labs 002–005, Mondays 9:05am–1:10pm, 1-250 Keller Hall |
| 010 | TuTh 4:00–5:15pm, 3-210 Keller Hall | Labs 011–014, Mondays 1:25pm–5:30pm, 1-250 Keller Hall |
## Prerequisites
Grade of **C- or better in CSCI 2021 or EE 2361**, admission to CS Major/Minor or CompE Major. Beyond the formal requirement: solid C is essential, not optional - if rusty, review before week 1 using the C Programming Resources section below.
## Learning Objectives
By the end: understand core OS abstractions (processes, files, threads, synchronization, communication, local and networked) and why they enable security, integrity, flexibility, and efficiency; understand parallelism/concurrency tradeoffs and write real multi-process/multi-thread programs; reason about distributed systems (replication, partitioning, consistency, consensus) and their network protocols; do real systems programming with OS/networking primitives, low-level in C and higher-level in Python.
## Textbooks
Both required: *Advanced Programming in the UNIX Environment*, 3rd ed., Stevens & Rago, 2013 (via CourseWorks); *Designing Data-Intensive Applications*, 1st ed., Kleppmann, 2017 (via Library Course Materials).
## Computing Environment
> [!WARNING] Docker setup is not optional and not extendable
> Every assignment gets graded inside a standardized Docker Linux environment - this gets set up in the first lab (Mon 2026-09-14). Environment problems are explicitly **not** grounds for a deadline extension, and neither is data loss - back up work regularly. Setup guidance lives in the course's Programming Environment Guide on Canvas, not reproduced here yet.
## Important Dates
Start of semester / first lecture: **Tue 2026-09-08**. First lab meetings: **Mon 2026-09-14**. Midterm 1: **Thu 2026-10-08**, in class, 75 min. Midterm 2: **Thu 2026-11-12**, in class, 75 min. Final exam - Section 001: **Mon 2026-12-21, 8:00–10:00am**, location TBD. Final exam - Section 010: **Thu 2026-12-17, 4:00–6:00pm**, location TBD.
## Course Tools
Canvas is the primary information source - check it regularly. Piazza is the Q&A forum. Gradescope handles all programming-assignment and exam submission/grading, including regrade requests.
## Grading
| Component | Weight |
|---|---|
| Weekly Canvas Quizzes (lowest 2 dropped) | 10% |
| Laboratory Assignments (lowest 2 dropped) | 10% |
| Course Projects | 25% |
| Midterm Exam 1 | 15% |
| Midterm Exam 2 | 15% |
| Final Exam (cumulative) | 20% |
| Surveys | 5% |
No rounding on the scale below; a curve, if applied, only moves grades up, never down.
| % | Grade | % | Grade |
|---|---|---|---|
| 93+ | A | 73–76 | C |
| 90–92 | A- | 70–72 | C- |
| 87–89 | B+ | 65–69 | D+ |
| 83–86 | B | 60–64 | D |
| 80–82 | B- | ≤59 | F |
| 77–79 | C+ | | |
## Labs
Attendance at each week's lab is required to get credit for that week's assignment - labs solidify lecture material and are the natural place to find project teammates. Submitted on Gradescope, due **11:59pm the Wednesday following** each lab session. **No late work accepted** for labs; two lowest scores dropped to absorb unavoidable misses.
## Weekly Canvas Quizzes
Posted weekly, applies recent lecture/lab/reading material, auto-graded with **10 attempts allowed**, due **Monday 11:59pm** each week. No late work accepted; two lowest dropped. Open-resource and open-collaboration, but the submitted answers must reflect real personal understanding - the academic integrity policy applies here too.
## Projects
Four projects this semester (see Schedule below), each pairing a coding submission with an **individual oral exam** on its design and functionality - even if the project itself was done as a team of two. Teams of three+ are not allowed, and once a two-person team dissolves mid-project, the remaining partner finishes solo - no new partner until the next project. Grading has three real components: automated Gradescope test results, the oral exam, and manual inspection of error-handling quality specifically. Project grade is your **latest submission**, not your best one.
> [!WARNING] Late project penalty schedule
> On time: full credit. Up to 24h late: **90%** of earned credit. Up to 48h late: **80%**. Beyond 48h: **0%**. An 80-point submission becomes 72 at 24h late, 64 at 48h late, 0 after that.
## Exams
Two 75-minute midterms during normal lecture time, one 120-minute cumulative final at the university-assigned time. All graded and regrade-requested through Gradescope. **Collaboration in any form is prohibited on exams**, and the default assumption is that no resources (notes, electronics, textbooks) are allowed unless a specific exam's resource policy says otherwise - that policy gets announced well ahead of each exam.
## Extra Credit, Withdrawals, Incompletes
No extra credit opportunities exist in this course. Withdrawal without special approval is only guaranteed through the end of Week 4 - after that, only the college administration can approve one, and only in extraordinary circumstances. Incompletes are instructor-discretion only, and only with a substantial, timely portion of the work already done.
## Academic Integrity & the AI Policy
> [!DANGER] This directly constrains how Claude Code gets used for this class
> Generative AI is allowed only for **learning concepts**, **investigating an error message**, or **generating a general example unrelated to your actual submission**. It is explicitly banned for producing any part of the code actually submitted for a lab or project - pasting a spec/tests into an AI tool and asking for a solution, asking it to implement one function of your submission, or asking it to fix a bug you found in your own code are all named as violations. "If unsure, assume it's not allowed and ask the instructor first." The course states it uses plagiarism-detection software resistant to obfuscation attempts, and any confirmed violation goes to the Office of Community Standards and will likely fail the course outright.
Standard academic-integrity violations also apply and are enforced the same way: copying any prior solution (including from a past semester), copying from any outside source, using an instructor solution manual, submitting someone else's work, paying for solutions, posting solutions publicly, collaborating on exams, and taking or giving code to another student - all explicitly named, all treated as academic dishonesty including during office hours.
## Workload
Per university policy, ~3 hours/week per credit - this is a 4-credit course, so budget **~12 hours/week** against [[10_Areas/Life/Plans/Fall 2026/Fall 2026 Plan|Fall 2026 Plan]]'s 80/20 daily split. That is the heaviest single-class load among the six Fall'26 courses and should be weighted accordingly when the week's One Hard Thing competes with a project deadline.
## C Programming Resources
Prerequisite review material, ranked by the course itself: **Dive Into Systems** (first few chapters, free online) is the top recommendation. Others if more depth is needed: the C Programming Wikibook, Beej's Guide to C Programming (informal, complete, ad-free), the GNU C Programming Tutorial (Burgess & Hale-Evans, a bit scattered), Learn C from ProgramIz (ad-heavy but example-rich), Burgess's 1999 C tutorial (dated but complete, single page), and Modern C (long, comprehensive, reflects current idiom).

# AMES 1201 — Arrow, Fist and Sword: Conceptions of the Hero in Asian Cultures
Fall'26, 3 credits. Closes the Arts/Humanities & Literature sub-requirement in Diversified Core — per [[APAS]] this is the last open Lib Ed bucket, so this class finishes Lib Ed outright. Full syllabus read directly from `D:\Users\_Anant\10_Areas\UMN\Classes\Lib Eds & More\AMES 1201\AMES 1201 Syllabus.pdf`, 2026-09-08.
## Source of truth
`D:\Users\_Anant\10_Areas\UMN\Classes\Lib Eds & More\AMES 1201` is the real folder for this course - syllabus, slides, readings, and any Canvas exports land there first, never in the vault directly. This note is the readable distillation, not a replacement for the primary files.
## What This Course Actually Covers
Concepts of the hero across Persian, Indian, Chinese, Korean, and Japanese cultures - how each defines the ethos of heroism and its community role, how the hero changes over time and under modern nationalism, what part gender roles play (and whether a "female" hero is possible within these traditions), and how popular film lets modern Asian societies reinterpret their own heroic traditions. Taught through five real thematic units, not abstract theory: **Persia** - Rostam's career in Ferdowsi's 11th-century *Shahnameh*, plus a modern Iranian animated retelling. **India** - the *Ramayana* via R. K. Narayan's prose adaptation, plus an anime-style India/Japan film co-production. **China** - solitary adventurers (*youxia*) in early narratives, then how that hero-type transferred to women through medieval woman-knight tales and the cross-dressing Mulan. **Korea** - the outlaw Hong Gildong, one of the most famous works of early modern Korean literature, plus a North Korean film adaptation. **Japan** - the first samurai portrayals in the 13th-century *Tales of the Heike*: codes of honor, warrior behavior, tragedy in the Japanese context.
## Instructor
**Morgan Rouzer** (prouzer@umn.edu) - professor of Chinese and Japanese literature (pre-modern specialty), Department of Asian and Middle Eastern Studies. Harvard BA (1982) and PhD (1989, East Asian Languages and Civilizations). Publishes on Chinese poetry; has worked since 2000 on Buddhist ideas in Chinese/Japanese literature and culture; also teaches Classical Chinese and is explicitly interested in how pre-modern East Asian culture gets redefined in modern manga/anime - directly relevant to why this course pairs classical texts with film adaptations rather than treating them separately.
## Meetings
**M, W: 1:00–2:15pm, Rapson Hall 100.**
## Required Reading
Four books, and the specific translations matter - the syllabus is explicit that other translations/adaptations "will be quite different in content and quality," so substituting one isn't a safe shortcut. **Abolqasem Ferdowsi**, *Rostam: Tales of Love and War from the Shahnameh* (Dick Davis translation, Penguin). **R. K. Narayan**, *The Ramayana: A Shortened Modern Prose Version of the Indian Epic* (Penguin). *The Story of Hong Gildong* (Penguin). **Burton Watson & Haruo Shirane**, *The Tales of the Heike* (Columbia). Additional short readings (including some of the instructor's own translations) get posted on Canvas as needed - check the front page regularly.
## Films
Six films/anime series, streamed via a Google Drive folder shared with enrolled students - **viewing only, no downloading allowed**: *The Last Fiction* (Iran, 2018); *Ramayana: The Legend of Prince Rama* (India/Japan, 1993); *Mulan Joins the Army* (China, 1939); *Mulan: Rise of a Warrior* (China, 2009); *Hong Kil Dong* (North Korea, 1986); *The Heike Story* (Japan, 2021, selected episodes).
## Class Attendance
No separate attendance grade, but lectures matter directly: assignment answers that suggest inattention to lecture get marked down for it. Lecture slides are posted as PDFs on Canvas after each session, and **you're responsible for everything on them** regardless of attendance.
## AI Policy
> [!WARNING] More permissive than CSCI 4061, but still a real line
> "I have no objection to using AI to help you refine your own ideas, [but] you should not be relying on AI to write the assignments for you." Unlike [[20_Progress/Degree/CSCI 4061/CSCI 4061 Board|CSCI 4061's]] near-total ban, this leaves room for AI-assisted brainstorming and refinement - but a full policy handout exists on Canvas beyond this one paragraph and hasn't been read yet. Don't assume "refine your ideas" covers more than it says until that handout gets checked.
## Workload
~8.5 hours/week for this 3-credit course: 2.5 hours instructional (both class meetings), 7.5 hours independent (reading/watching, reference lookups, discussion-question prep, Canvas homework, writing assignments) - wait, that adds to 10, not 8.5; the syllabus states both the 8.5-hour total and the 2.5+7.5 breakdown, which don't reconcile on their own math. Treat 8.5 hours/week as the intended real figure and the 7.5-hour independent-work number as the one to trust for planning, since instructional time is fixed by the two weekly class meetings. Either way, this is the lightest of the six Fall'26 courses by a wide margin against [[10_Areas/Life/Plans/Fall 2026/Fall 2026 Plan|Fall 2026 Plan]]'s 80/20 split.
## Grading
Three components, adding to 100 points, no rounding (92.99 stays an A-, never rounds to 93).
| Component | Points |
|---|---|
| Canvas quizzes (5, one per unit, 5 pts each) | 25 |
| Take-home exercises (5, one per unit, 10 pts each) | 50 |
| Short-answer take-home final | 25 |
| % | Grade | % | Grade |
|---|---|---|---|
| 93–100 | A | 73–76.99 | C |
| 90–92.99 | A- | 70–72.99 | C- |
| 87–89.99 | B+ | 67–69.99 | D+ |
| 83–86.99 | B | 60–66.99 | D |
| 80–82.99 | B- | ≤59.99 | F |
| 77–79.99 | C+ | | |
No extra credit exists in this course.
## Due Dates
**2026-10-11** (Su): Persia unit exercise + quiz. **2026-10-25** (Su): India unit exercise + quiz. **2026-11-15** (Su): China unit exercise + quiz. **2026-12-01** (Tu): Korea unit exercise + quiz. **2026-12-20** (Su): Japan unit exercise + quiz. **2026-12-23** (W): final short-answer take-home due - the semester's real hard deadline. The instructor states she "generally does not penalize" lateness before that final cutoff, but a firm end-of-semester deadline still applies - don't treat the no-penalty language as unlimited.
## Schedule
Unit assignment instructions get distributed partway through each unit, not on day one - watch for that, not just the reading list.
| Unit | Dates | Topic | Key Readings/Screenings |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1: Persia | 9/9–9/30 | Intro; Ferdowsi's *Shahnameh* - Rostam's origin, his Seven Trials, the tragedy of Sohrab, the story of Zahhak; closes with *The Last Fiction* | "The Love of Zal and Rudabeh" (due 9/16), "The Seven Trials of Rostam" (9/21), "The Tragedy of Sohrab" (9/23), Zahhak excerpt (9/28) |
| 2: India | 10/5–10/14 | Background to the *Ramayana*; Narayan's prose version in two parts; anime-style film adaptation | Narayan Chs 1–6 (due 10/7), Chs 7–14 (10/12), *Ramayana: The Legend of Prince Rama* screening (10/14) |
| 3: China | 10/19–11/4 | Historical background; medieval adventurers/thieves; Mulan in history, poetry, drama, and two film versions | *Medieval Chinese Tales of Adventurers* selected stories (due 10/21 & 10/26), *Mulan Joins the Army* (11/2), *Mulan: Rise of a Warrior* (11/4) |
| 4: Korea | 11/9–11/18 | Background lecture; Hong Gildong's rise and outlaw career in two parts; North Korean film retelling | *The Story of Hong Gildong* through 47% (due 11/11), remainder (11/16), *Hong Kil Dong* screening (11/18) |
| 5: Japan | 11/23–12/16 | Background on samurai Japan; *Tales of the Heike* - Giō, the Bridge battle, Nara's burning, Kiyomori's death, Ichi-no-Tani buildup, Dan-no-ura buildup; anime adaptation; aftermath and Yoshitsune's fall; class conclusions. **No class 11/25.** | Heike excerpts (due 11/30 and 12/2), Dan-no-ura buildup excerpts (12/7), *The Heike Story* screening (12/9) |
Due-date rows for each unit's exercise/quiz are in Due Dates above, not repeated in this table.
## Why This Course Counts Toward the Degree (Lib Ed Rationale)
Fulfills two Liberal Education requirements at once, per [[APAS]]: the **Literature Core** (close reading of heroic narrative forms - epic, saga - and how cultural values get encoded in them) and the **Global Perspectives Theme** (engaging non-Western conceptions of heroism on their own terms, then tracing how those conceptions shift under modern nationalism and film). The course's own framing: understanding why a culture's heroes act as they do is a real step toward understanding that culture's values, not just a literature exercise.
## Policies (Standard UMN Boilerplate)
Standard University policies apply and aren't reproduced here in full - Student Conduct Code, scholastic dishonesty (plagiarism, unauthorized collaboration, falsified records - can result in an F or N for the course), makeup work for legitimate absences (illness, subpoena, military service, bereavement, religious observance - voting is explicitly *not* covered), personal electronic device use at instructor discretion, disability accommodations via the DRC, Title IX/sexual misconduct reporting, and academic freedom. Check the source PDF directly if any of these specifics matter for a real situation.

CSCI 4511W, Introduction to Artificial Intelligence, Fall 2026 (001 and 010)
This course, according to the course catalog is:

"Agents. Problem-solving using search algorithms. Knowledge representation and inference using formal logic. Knowledge graphs. Planning. Introduction to machine learning."

By the end of this course, you should

Have a deep understanding of classical search algorithms, both analysis and implementation
Know when to apply uninformed and informed search strategies
Be able to apply search strategies for constraint problems and adversarial environments
Understand how computer systems represent knowledge
Know how to write academic papers
Section Information
This Canvas site will be used to support two sections of this course.

Section 001
Lecture Times: MW 4 - 5:15pm

Location: Bruininks Hall 220

Section 010
Lecture Times: MW 1 - 2:15pm

Location: Vincent Hall 16

Course Staff
Instructor: Dr. Andy Exley exle0002@umn.edu

Graduate TAs (Discussion Section leads): 

Maryam Kameli (kamel026@umn.edu)

London Lowmanstone (lowma016@umn.edu)

Zephaniah Johnson (joh15514@umn.edu)

Undergraduate TAs (Grading):

NZ Gorham (gorha079@umn.edu)

Materials
Textbook: Stuart Russell and Peter Norvig, "Artificial Intelligence. A modern approach. 4th Edition" Pearson, 2020.  (Chapters 1-12).

Grading
4 Problem Sets - (Worth a total of 36%)
4 Writing Assignments - (Each worth 3% of your grade for a total of 12%)
12-14 Short Quizzes - (Each worth around 1% of your grade for a total of 12%)
One Project - Worth 10% of your grade
Discussion Attendance and Participation - Worth 6% of your grade
3 Long Quizzes - (Each worth 8% of your grade for a total of 24%)
Using the above weights, a total of 90% and up will earn you some level of A, 80% and up at least some level of B, 70% and up at least some level of C, 60% and up at least some level of D.

Problem Sets
Problem sets will consist of 5-6 written or coding problems that you will be required to complete individually and turn in online. Assessment for the coding portions may require you to meet with a TA and complete a code review. Code reviews must be completed within 10 days of the assignment due date.

Writing Assignments
Writing assignments will build up to learning how to write in an academic style. You will write papers as though they were for publication as research, which means that you will learn a specific format and ways of citing sources, some of which is unique to the field of Computer Science.

Discussion Section Attendance and Participation
Attendance is required in your discussion sections, and you will be graded on your attendance and participation. Discussion sections will focus on reading, discussing and writing research papers. 

Short Quizzes
Short quizzes will be given via gradescope and cover recent material. You may work with your classmates or consult the textbook or notes while taking them. They are due at 6pm on the day they are assigned, it is recommended that you complete them in class.

Long Quizzes 
Long quizzes will be given in person. They are individual efforts, you may not work with your classmates. You may consult your notes (2 pages max) while taking them. The time allowed to take them will be limited to 45 minutes.

Late Policy
Short quizzes, problem sets, and writing assignments that are turned in up to one day late will receive a penalty (usually 10%, or a fixed amount for in-class exercises). Work later than one day late will not be accepted by gradescope, and you'll have to contact thptember 202627

# CSCI 3081W — Program Design and Development
> [!WARNING] Dropped 2026-09-09
> This course was dropped for Fall'26 and replaced by [[20_Progress/Degree/CSCI 4511W/CSCI 4511W Board|CSCI 4511W]] (Intro to AI). Everything below is preserved as real, syllabus-sourced material - not deleted - because it was a genuine CS Core + upper-division WI requirement and may matter again for Spring'27/Fall'27 planning. See [[Fall'26 Syllabus]]'s Path to Graduation section for the resulting degree-requirement gap this drop creates.
==Full syllabus captured 2026-09-08, pasted directly from Canvas.== Closes the Program Design & Development sub-requirement of the Computer Science Core and is the major's upper-division WI course, per [[APAS]] - it does **not** close the broader Writing Intensive Lib Ed bucket on its own, one more upper-division WI course is still needed there.
## Source of Truth
`D:\Users\_Anant\10_Areas\UMN\Classes\CSCI\CSCI 3081` is the real folder for this course, still empty as of 2026-09-08 - this syllabus came from pasted Canvas text, not a downloaded PDF. Desktop shortcut: `CSCI 3081.lnk` in `D:\Users\_Anant\Inbox\`.
## Catalog Info
Course number CSCI 3081W, **Program Design and Development**, Fall 2026, 4 credits. Two sections ran under this syllabus.
## Instructor & Course Staff
**Professor Liia Butler** - butle547@umn.edu - 322 Lind Hall, 207 Church St SE, Minneapolis MN 55455.
*Graduate TAs:* Sam Adeniyi (adeni026@umn.edu), Tongyu Nie (nie00035@umn.edu), Walter Sands (sands224@umn.edu).
*Undergraduate TAs:* Yassir Abdalla, Arbor Garnett, Maxwell Halbur, King Hoo, Jackson Kirchner, Raghav Mundada, Nandhini Sridhar, Karthik Sriram.
The instructor delivers lectures, sometimes assisted by undergrad TAs. Grad TAs oversee labs and help there; undergrad TAs also help in labs. Lab meetings get video demonstrations when applicable.
## Course Links
- Canvas Section 001: https://canvas.umn.edu/courses/579173
- Canvas Section 010: https://canvas.umn.edu/courses/579223
- Gradescope: https://www.gradescope.com/courses/1330310 - enrollment code **6XDWNZ**
- Piazza: https://piazza.com/class/mtga6txdip45z6
- GitHub Organization: https://github.com/CSCI3081WFa26
## Meetings
| Section | Lecture | Labs (Friday) |
|---|---|---|
| 001 | Tue/Thu 4:00-5:15 PM, Bruininks Hall 114 | Lab 001 F 10:10-11:00am · Lab 002 F 11:15am-12:05pm · Lab 003 F 12:20-1:10pm · Lab 004 F 2:30-3:20pm - all in Keller Hall 1-250 |
| 010 | Mon/Wed 9:45-11:00 AM, Jackson Hall 2-137 | Lab 011 F 9:05-9:55am · Lab 012 F 10:10-11:00am · Lab 013 F 11:15am-12:05pm - all in Keller Hall 1-262 |
## General Information
Theory, skills, and tools to become a good software developer - preparation for 4xxx/5xxx-level programming-intensive courses. Topics: software development process, professional skills, and writing in computer science. **Java** is the language used throughout.
## Role of Writing
Writing-intensive by design: **Software Documentation** (codebase intros plus Javadoc API docs), **UML Diagrams**, a **team policies document**, **self/team feedback** after each iteration, and **code reviews**.
## Expected Effort
12-15 hours/week average - large-material, writing-intensive, not casual.
## Prerequisites
*Formal:* (CSCI 2021, CSCI 2041, CSci upper division) or CSci grad standing, or department consent. *Expected skill:* prior OOP experience (Java, C++, or Python), basic data structures and algorithms.
## Optional Textbooks
No required textbook. Suggested: *Code Complete*, *Head First Design Patterns*, *Head First Java*, *Pro Git*.
## Assessments Overview
*Participation:* offline Gradescope assessment or live in-class, two lowest scores auto-dropped. *Labs:* 9 graded, individual, peer help allowed but must be cited. *Quizzes:* 6, proctored, 45-minute limit, lowest dropped. *Project Iterations:* 3, teams of three, deliverables plus individual code review. *Exams:* none.
## Grading
| Component | Weight |
|---|---|
| Syllabus Quiz | 1% |
| Participation Assignments | 8% |
| Labs | 15% |
| Quizzes | 25% |
| Project Iteration 1 (10% deliverables + 7% code review) | 17% |
| Project Iteration 2 (10% deliverables + 7% code review) | 17% |
| Project Iteration 3 (10% deliverables + 7% code review) | 17% |
*Scale (absolute, no curve, no rounding):* A 93+ · A- 90-<93 · B+ 87-<90 · B 83-<87 · B- 80-<83 · C+ 77-<80 · C 73-<77 · C- 70-<73 · D+ 67-<70 · D 60-<67 · F <60.
## Submission & Late Work Policy
> [!WARNING] Code must compile on CSE ITLabs Linux machines - not just locally
> Code that doesn't compile there gets zero credit regardless of local success. Late work not accepted at all - cushion is the automatic drops instead.
## Project Details
**Project topic that semester: a visual simulation and visualization of a transportation system**, extended across three iterations with software design patterns, live visualization, and result analysis.
## Weekly Schedule (Definitive Dates)

| 1 | Mon 9/7 - Fri 9/11 | Lecture 01: Course welcome | Lecture 02: Version control systems | Lab 01: Git |

# CSCI 3081W — Preparation
> [!WARNING] Dropped 2026-09-09 - preserved as reference, see [[CSCI 3081W Board]]
==The three Project Iterations are 51% of the grade combined, and each one pairs a team deliverable with an individually-scored code review - team function matters as much as code quality here.==
## Where the Points Actually Are
Project Iterations 1-3 (17% each = 51%) dwarf everything else - Quizzes (25%), Labs (15%), Participation (8%), and the Syllabus Quiz (1%) are the remaining 49% combined. Each iteration splits into 10% deliverables and 7% code review, and code reviews are scored individually even on a team submission - a strong team result with a weak individual code-review performance still costs real points.
## Cheapest Points
The Syllabus Quiz (1%) and Participation Assignments (8%, two lowest auto-dropped) are close to free if the reading and lecture-follow-up habit is kept up.
## The Real Traps
- **Code must compile on the CSE ITLabs Linux machines, not just locally** - zero credit if it doesn't.
- **No late work, period** - the built-in cushion is the automatic drops (two lowest participation, lowest lab, lowest quiz) instead.
- **No stated AI policy** - would have needed confirming on Canvas or Piazza before this course was dropped.
- **Quizzes are proctored, 45 minutes, zero outside resources**, and discussing quiz content while any window is open is a stated academic-integrity violation.
## Study Cadence
Quizzes ran roughly every other week covering the prior two weeks. For project weeks (Weeks 6, 9, 13), the mandatory-attendance lecture and lab sessions had no late-work fallback.
## Open Question
- [ ] None - archived, no longer active.

# CSCI 4511W — Introduction to Artificial Intelligence
Fall'26, replaces the dropped [[20_Progress/Degree/CSCI 3081W/CSCI 3081W Board|CSCI 3081W]] as of 2026-09-09. Full syllabus text pasted directly from Canvas, 2026-09-09. Degree-requirement impact of this swap - what's confirmed closed vs. now genuinely uncertain - is tracked in [[Fall'26 Syllabus]]'s Path to Graduation section, not repeated here.
## Source of Truth
`D:\Users\_Anant\10_Areas\UMN\Classes\CSCI\CSCI 4511W` is the real folder for this course, empty as of 2026-09-09 - this syllabus came from pasted Canvas text, not a downloaded PDF or a full week-by-week calendar. Save an actual copy of the syllabus and future slides/assignments there.
## Catalog Info
Course number CSCI 4511W, **Introduction to Artificial Intelligence**, Fall 2026, two sections (001 and 010) sharing one Canvas site. Catalog description: "Agents. Problem-solving using search algorithms. Knowledge representation and inference using formal logic. Knowledge graphs. Planning. Introduction to machine learning."
## Learning Objectives
By the end: deep understanding of classical search algorithms (both analysis and implementation); know when to apply uninformed vs. informed search strategies; apply search strategies to constraint problems and adversarial environments; understand how computer systems represent knowledge; know how to write academic papers.
## Instructor & Course Staff
**Dr. Andy Exley** - exle0002@umn.edu.
*Graduate TAs (Discussion Section leads):* Maryam Kameli (kamel026@umn.edu), London Lowmanstone (lowma016@umn.edu), Zephaniah Johnson (joh15514@umn.edu).
*Undergraduate TAs (Grading):* Banu Arunachalm (aruna019@umn.edu), NZ Gorham (gorha079@umn.edu).
## Meetings
| Section | Lecture | Location |
|---|---|---|
| 001 | MW 4:00–5:15pm | Bruininks Hall 220 |
| 010 | MW 1:00–2:15pm | Vincent Hall 16 |
Discussion sections (led by the Grad TAs) run separately from lecture - specific times not in the pasted syllabus text, confirm on Canvas.
## Textbook
**Stuart Russell & Peter Norvig**, *Artificial Intelligence: A Modern Approach*, 4th ed., Pearson 2020 - Chapters 1–12.
## Grading
| Component | Weight |
|---|---|
| Problem Sets (4) | 36% |
| Writing Assignments (4, 3% each) | 12% |
| Short Quizzes (12–14, ~1% each) | 12% |
| Long Quizzes (3, 8% each) | 24% |
| Final Project | 10% |
| Discussion Attendance & Participation | 6% |
| Recommended Readings | 0% (ungraded, tracked as a category anyway) |
Scale as stated: 90%+ for some level of A, 80%+ for some level of B, 70%+ for some level of C, 60%+ for some level of D - no finer +/- breakdown given in the pasted text.
## Problem Sets
5–6 written or coding problems each, completed and submitted individually online. Coding portions may require meeting a TA for a code review, which **must happen within 10 days of the assignment due date**.
## Writing Assignments
Build toward writing in academic style, as though for publication as research - a specific format and citation conventions unique to Computer Science, taught progressively across the four assignments.
## Discussion Section Attendance and Participation
Required and graded, not optional. Discussion sections focus on reading, discussing, and writing research papers - this is where the "know how to write academic papers" learning objective actually gets practiced.
## Short Quizzes
Via Gradescope, covering recent material. **Open-collaboration and open-resource** - classmates, textbook, and notes are all fair game. Due 6:00pm the day assigned; recommended to complete them in class.
## Long Quizzes
In person, individual effort only - **no collaboration allowed**. Up to 2 pages of personal notes permitted. **45-minute time limit.**
## Late Policy
> [!WARNING] This section is genuinely incomplete
> The pasted syllabus text cuts off mid-sentence here - a calendar widget's date text bled into the copy ("...you'll have to contact th[...]ptember 202627..."). What's clear: short quizzes, problem sets, and writing assignments turned in up to one day late get a penalty (usually 10%, or a fixed amount for in-class exercises); anything later than one day is **not accepted by Gradescope**, and requires contacting course staff directly - but the exact contact process and any exceptions weren't captured. Confirm the real policy on Canvas before relying on this.

Pulling the real Canvas schedule and the complete late-work section is this note's open task.
