# CSCI 4511W — Preparation
==Problem Sets and Long Quizzes together are 60% of the grade, and the Long Quizzes are the only fully closed-collaboration assessment in the course - everything else explicitly allows working with classmates or the textbook.==
## Where the Points Actually Are
Problem Sets (36%) and Long Quizzes (24%) are 60% combined - Short Quizzes (12%), Writing (12%), Final Project (10%), and Discussion Attendance (6%) make up the rest. The real asymmetry: Short Quizzes are open-book and open-collaboration, but Long Quizzes are individual-only with a hard 45-minute limit and a 2-page note cap - treat them as the closest thing this course has to a proctored exam, not as "just another quiz."
## Cheapest Points
Discussion Attendance & Participation (6%) and the Recommended Readings category (0% but tracked) are the lowest-effort-to-secure lines - showing up and engaging in discussion sections captures the 6% with no separate assessment to prepare for.
## The Real Traps
- **Coding problem-set portions may require a TA code review within 10 days of the due date** - missing that window is a real, separate deadline from the assignment due date itself, easy to lose track of if only the submission deadline gets calendared.
- **Short Quizzes and Long Quizzes have opposite collaboration rules** - Short Quizzes are open-resource and open-collaboration; Long Quizzes are individual-only with collaboration during the quiz window treated as a violation. Don't let Short Quiz habits carry into Long Quiz week.
- **The late-work policy is only partially known** - per [[CSCI 4511W Board]]'s Verification Notes, the pasted syllabus cuts off mid-sentence on what happens past the one-day-late window beyond "Gradescope won't accept it." Confirm the real process before assuming any flexibility exists.
- **This course replaced [[20_Progress/Degree/CSCI 3081W/CSCI 3081W Board|CSCI 3081W]] for a real degree requirement** - whether it closes the same major upper-division WI bucket 3081W did is unconfirmed; see [[Fall'26 Syllabus]]'s Path to Graduation section before assuming the degree math is unaffected.
## Study Cadence
With 12-14 Short Quizzes across the semester (roughly one a week), the natural cadence is: read the assigned Russell & Norvig chapter before its lecture, since Short Quizzes are described as covering "recent material" and are open-book - the prep that matters is having the material available and understood, not memorized. For the three Long Quizzes, budget real closed-book review time beforehand, since the open-collaboration habit built for weekly quizzes doesn't transfer.
## Open Question

- [ ] Get the complete late-work policy - the pasted text cuts off mid-sentence.

# ENGL 1004 (003) — Banned Books
Fall'26, replaces the dropped [[20_Progress/Degree/AMES 1201/AMES 1201 Board|AMES 1201]] as of 2026-09-09 - both close the same Lib Ed Literature sub-requirement; this one was picked as the easier fit despite having two lectures a week instead of AMES 1201's two. Full syllabus read directly from `D:\Users\_Anant\10_Areas\UMN\Classes\Lib Eds & More\ENGL 1004\ENGL 1004-003 syllabus fall 2026.pdf`, 2026-09-09.
## Source of Truth
`D:\Users\_Anant\10_Areas\UMN\Classes\Lib Eds & More\ENGL 1004` is the real folder for this course - syllabus, slides, readings, and any Canvas exports land there first.
> [!WARNING] Read this before enrolling attention gets assumed
> The syllabus opens with its own content warning, stated directly: "it is likely that you will find one or more of the class texts offensive and almost certain that you will find one or more uncomfortable and difficult to read... If you will be triggered by difficult, graphic, or offensive material in a way that will prohibit your class participation or completion of assignments you SHOULD NOT enroll in this course." Not boilerplate - the reading list includes *The Handmaid's Tale*, *The Bluest Eye*, and *Maus*, among others actually challenged/banned in real school districts.
## What This Course Actually Covers
A course about book banning, taught by reading the actual banned/challenged books alongside real First Amendment case law and state legislation. Not literary analysis in the abstract - the course culminates in a **live school-board simulation** where every student role-plays a real stakeholder (school board member, a parent aligned with Moms for Liberty, a community member opposed to any banning, a local reporter, a lawyer) arguing over three specific challenged titles: *And Tango Makes Three* (elementary read-aloud), *Melissa* (5th/6th grade classroom libraries), and *The Bluest Eye* (high school curriculum).
## Instructor & TA
**Professor Lianna Farber** (farbe004@umn.edu) - Pillsbury Hall 320I, office hours TTh 8:30–9:30am and by appointment.
**TA Sachiko Hamada** (hamad046@umn.edu) - Pillsbury Hall 220, office hours by appointment (T 1:30–3:00pm / Th 1:30–2:30pm).
## Meetings
**TTh 9:45–11:00am, Pillsbury Hall 214.**
## Required Texts
Eight books, plus Canvas/linked readings: Margaret Atwood, *The Handmaid's Tale*. Alison Bechdel, *Fun House*. Alex Gino, *Melissa* (originally published as *George* - either edition works). Toni Morrison, *The Bluest Eye*. Justin Richardson & Peter Parnell, *And Tango Makes Three*. Art Spiegelman, *Maus I and II*. Raina Telgemeier, *Drama*. Mark Twain, *Adventures of Huckleberry Finn*. A dedicated physical journal/notebook is also required (a 99-cent composition notebook is explicitly recommended) - journal entries must be handwritten, not typed.
## Class Requirements & Materials
Bring the assigned reading, a pen/pencil, and paper to every class. Electronic devices stay away unless specifically invited out. **Journal entries are due at least 1 hour before class** on assigned days, submitted as a photo or scan to Canvas.
## Grading
| Component | Weight |
|---|---|
| Journal Entries (12 total) | 30% |
| Role Creation | 10% |
| Simulation | 20% |
| Class Participation (excluding simulation) | 20% |
| Final Exam | 20% |
No extra credit is mentioned anywhere in this syllabus. Scale: standard UMN A (4.0) through F, S equivalent to C- or better - see Policies below for the full breakdown.
## Journal Entries
12 entries across the semester, handwritten, graded on a 0–2 scale per entry: **2** = well-considered, genuinely explores an issue the text raises; **1** = insufficiently considered, or late by up to a week; **0** = not submitted, or more than a week late. Early entries (1–3) are written "in your own voice" - real reactions with concrete textual evidence, not just feelings. Entries 4–6 and beyond shift to writing *as your assigned simulation character* - reasoning the way that character would, not as yourself.
## Role Creation & Simulation
Roles get assigned after a preference questionnaire, then fleshed out in class with a background and personal objectives - once set, **you're expected to stick to them** through the simulation. The simulation itself runs across three class sessions (10/8, 10/13, 10/20) as a school-board meeting on the three challenged titles, followed by a debrief on media reporting (10/22). Grading is based on how well you hold your objectives and how engaged you are - only students assigned to report on the proceedings may use a computer or electronic note-taking device during it.
## Class Participation
Explicitly **not the same as attendance** - graded on engagement and intellectual risk-taking. Being physically present but visibly disengaged scores a 0 for that day, same as an absence would for attendance purposes.
## Attendance
Mandatory, with an escalating penalty structure: missing more than **one cumulative week** unexcused lowers your grade; missing **three cumulative weeks** unexcused fails the course outright. Coming to class unprepared can get you asked to leave, counted as an unexcused absence.
## Electronic Devices
No use in class except when specifically invited. First violation gets a verbal warning; the **second violation is an automatic 0 for that day's participation grade** - stated as a deliberate policy, not an idle threat, with the instructor citing both anecdotal and research-based effects on retention.
## Final Exam
One in-class exam on the last scheduled day (**2026-12-15**) - passage identification from the readings, then discussion connecting the passage to the larger work and to the semester's discussions.
## AI Policy
> [!DANGER] The strictest AI ban of any Fall'26 course
> "Generative AI is NOT allowed for this class," stated in full caps in the syllabus itself - not a soft preference. The instructor's own reasoning: AI "spits out the most obvious takes only," including incorrect ones, simplifies and smooths over subtlety, and blocks the actual skill the course is teaching (working through a difficult text to your own opinion). The syllabus states a specific detection method - the instructor has tested the actual readings/assignments against AI models and predicts what AI-assisted work looks like: mediocre (low-B) class contributions, poor exam performance, a shallow simulation role, or a mismatch between real-time class performance and written work quality. **If AI use is suspected, the consequence is a standing requirement to discuss readings weekly and write journal entries during the instructor's office hours** - not a one-time penalty, an ongoing supervision arrangement. Separately, **plagiarism explicitly includes AI-generated submissions** and can fail the assignment or the course outright.
## Schedule
The simulation block (10/8, 10/13, 10/20) has no separate reading beyond planning; specific journal-entry numbers are noted where the syllabus assigns them.

## Policies (Standard UMN Boilerplate, Plus Course-Specific Additions)
Standard University policies apply (Student Conduct Code, disability accommodations via the DRC, Title IX/sexual misconduct reporting, mental health resources, academic freedom) - not reproduced here in full. Course-specific additions worth flagging: a **class masking request** if feeling under the weather (not mandatory, requested); the course is scheduled in-person but may shift to Zoom/recorded for extreme weather, instructor illness, or a significant number of ill students; no class recordings will be made "at the request of individual students." Full A-F grade scale: A 4.000 · A- 3.667 · B+ 3.333 · B 3.000 · B- 2.667 · C+ 2.333 · C 2.000 · C- 1.667 · D+ 1.333 · D 1.000 · S equivalent to C- or better.

# ENGL 1004 (003) — Preparation
==Journal Entries (30%) and Class Participation (20%) together are half the grade, and both are graded on engagement quality, not correctness - this is the opposite of a course where showing up quietly is enough.==
## Where the Points Actually Are
Journal Entries (30%), Simulation (20%), and Participation (20%) together are 70% of the grade - Role Creation (10%) and the Final Exam (20%) are the remaining 30%. Unlike a typical lit course, there's no single high-stakes paper - the grade is built almost entirely from sustained, real engagement across the semester rather than a few big deliverables.
## Cheapest Points
There isn't really a free-points category here - even attendance-adjacent credit (Class Participation) explicitly requires visible engagement, not just presence, per [[ENGL 1004 Board]]. The closest thing to a forgiving margin: Journal Entries score a 1 (not a 0) for being late up to a week, so a missed entry isn't catastrophic if made up promptly.
## The Real Traps
- **Generative AI is completely banned, with real detection reasoning behind it** - the instructor has tested course material against AI models and describes exactly what AI-assisted work looks like (mediocre class contributions, weak exam performance, shallow simulation roles). If suspected, the consequence isn't a one-time penalty - it's a standing weekly requirement to discuss readings and write journal entries under supervision during office hours. This is the strictest AI policy of any Fall'26 course - see [[ENGL 1004 Board]]'s AI Policy section.
- **Attendance has a hard failure threshold, not just a grade penalty**: one cumulative unexcused week lowers the grade, three cumulative weeks fails the course outright.
- **Electronic device policy has real teeth**: first phone-out gets a warning, second gets an automatic 0 for that day's participation - stated as deliberate, not an idle threat.
- **Journal entries must be handwritten and submitted as a photo/scan**, due at least 1 hour before class - a typed entry or a late-night submission the morning of class both fail the format/timing requirement even if the content is strong.
- **Content warning is real, not procedural** - the reading list includes *The Handmaid's Tale*, *The Bluest Eye*, and *Maus*, texts genuinely challenged/banned in real districts, and the syllabus states outright that students who'd be unable to participate around difficult material shouldn't be in the course.
## Study Cadence
Buy all 8 required texts immediately - the first reading (*Huckleberry Finn* ch. 1-6) is due 9/15, only a week into the semester, and the pace doesn't let up. The simulation block (10/8, 10/13, 10/20) is the structural center of the course - role creation work done well before 10/1 (when the revised role sketch is due) pays off across three full class sessions plus the debrief, not just one assignment.
## Open Question
- [ ] None outstanding - the syllabus was read in full and no gaps were found beyond normal per-semester confirmations (exact office-hour room changes, etc.).

# CSCI 4511W — Textbook Map
==Russell & Norvig's *Artificial Intelligence: A Modern Approach* (4th ed.) is the stated textbook covering Chapters 1-12, but only Chapters 8-9 have a confirmed reading-assignment date so far - and a separate set of "Chapter 7" readings almost certainly belongs to a different, uncited book, not this one.== Cross-referenced against the Schedule section in [[CSCI 4511W Board]].
## Russell & Norvig — Artificial Intelligence: A Modern Approach (4th ed., Pearson 2020)
The syllabus states this text covers Chapters 1-12 overall. Only the following chapters have an actual reading-assignment date, captured from Canvas Modules 2026-09-09:
- **Ch 8.1-8.2** - due Nov 30, inside the course's own "First-Order Logic" module.
- **Ch 8.3** - due Dec 2, same module.
- **Ch 9.1-9.2** - due Dec 7, same module.
- **Ch 9.3** - due Dec 9, same module.
Two independent confirmations that this mapping is real, not guessed: Russell & Norvig's own table of contents has Chapter 8 as *First-Order Logic* and Chapter 9 as *Inference in First-Order Logic*, and the course's own Canvas module covering these exact readings is literally named "First-Order Logic." None of these four readings has an individual chapter note yet - each gets created once its week is actually covered in lecture, per [[Course Week Standard]].
## Chapters 1-6, 10-12 (In the Textbook's Stated Range, No Reading Dates Captured Yet)
The syllabus's "Chapters 1-12" claim covers these too, but no reading assignment for Chapters 1-6, 10, 11, or 12 has been pulled from Canvas - [[CSCI 4511W Board]]'s Schedule section only covers Nov 16 through Dec 14, and none of that range names these chapters. The catalog description's topic order (agents, search algorithms, knowledge representation, planning, intro to ML) makes it plausible these map to the September-through-mid-November weeks not yet pulled, but that is an inference from the catalog description, not a confirmed schedule mapping - do not treat it as one.
## An Unresolved Second Source: "Chapter 7" and "Vector Semantics"
Three reading assignments don't fit the Russell & Norvig mapping above:
- **Chapter 7.3-7.4** - due Nov 18, inside a module whose name was captured truncated as "Approaches: Vector Semantics" (almost certainly "Modern Approaches: Vector Semantics," cut off by a scroll boundary in the paste).
- **Chapter 7.5-7.6** - due Nov 23, same module.
- **"Reading: Vector Semantics"** - due Dec 14, no chapter number given, inside the fully-named "Modern Approaches: Vector Semantics" module.
Russell & Norvig's own Chapter 7 is **Logical Agents** - it has nothing to do with vector semantics or embeddings. Vector semantics is a standard topic in NLP-specific textbooks (for example, Jurafsky & Martin's *Speech and Language Processing*), not in AIMA's core chapters. This strongly suggests these three readings come from a **second, uncited course text**, not from Russell & Norvig - the same flag already recorded in [[CSCI 4511W Board]]'s Schedule section. **Do not create a "Chapter - 7.md" note under Russell & Norvig for these** until the actual source book is confirmed on Canvas or Piazza - filing them under the wrong text would misattribute the reading and corrupt this map.
## Standard
Each chapter note, once created, follows [[Textbook Template]] - one highlight anchor, bolded key concepts, a worked example, a connection back to the matching lecture, and flashcards.
## Status
Zero chapter notes written as of 2026-09-09. Of the textbook's 12 stated chapters, only 2 (Chapters 8 and 9, each split across two reading dates) have a confirmed assignment date. The other 8 chapters (1-6, 10-12) and the identity of the second source behind the vector-semantics readings are both real open gaps, not oversights - see [[CSCI 4511W Board]]'s own `next:` field for the plan to close the wider schedule gap this map depends on.

MGMT 3015:
Introduction to Entrepreneurship 
Sec 001, Fall 2026, 4 credits

September 8th - December 16th, 2026, Mondays and Wednesdays, 2:30 - 4:10pm in Hanson Hall 1-111

Course Instructor
Shaker A. Zahra
Professor
Strategic Management & Entrepreneurship Dept.
University of Minnesota, Twin Cities
Carlson School of Management

Email: zahra004@umn.edu
Phone: (612) 702 1666 (Cell)
Office: 3-390 Marilyn C. Nelson Hall
Office Hours: M/W 4-5 & by appointment 

Teaching Assistant
Mr. Wayne Chou
PhD Candidate
Email: chou0211@umn.edu
Office:  Marilyn C. Nelson Hall 4-169
Office Hours: TBD

Course Goals & Overview
In recent years, the quest for entrepreneurial success has become increasingly complex.  Rapid-fire technological changes and highly permeable global markets are just two of the many forces that contribute to this dynamic.  Yet,   the basic principles remain the same.  These basics are best learned by simply knocking on doors, rolling up your sleeves, and applying a fair amount of “elbow grease.”  Therefore, the objectives for this semester are as follows:

To develop your capacity to generate business ideas, filter them, and recognize the most feasible and potentially profitable opportunities amongst all of the noise present in today’s marketplace.
To improve your ability to champion your best ideas in writing and in face-to-face interactions with important stakeholders – investors/lenders, customers/community, suppliers/partners and current/future members of your own team.
To help you determine the most effective business model and strategies for moving your ideas from concept to reality.
To give you a taste of how founders build startups – from basic research, product/service design and founding team formation to an initial sales push.
Teaching Approach
This introductory course is designed to help you build a foundation of knowledge, tools and techniques used by today’s entrepreneurs. We will explore these topics through the use of your textbooks, case studies, team projects, small group interaction with the instructor, as well as occasional guest speakers from the community.

Required Textbook and Reading Materials
Two main references are required for this course: a textbook (which covers readings) and course packet (which contains cases)

Entrepreneurship by Andrew Zacharakis, William D. Bygrave, Andrew C. Corbett 
Ed. (ISBN: 978-1-119-56309-9), 2019, 512 pages

Additional material (including short cases) will be posted on Canvas
Inclusive Access eTextbook is available through Canvas

An email will be sent to all students with the subject line "Important Course Materials Info: Charges to Your Student Account". This email will introduce them to the inclusive access program and will also give them instructions on how to opt out if they want to.
The original email comes from no-reply@verbasoftware.com so it sometimes goes to spam.
Please e-mail inclusiveaccess@umn.edu directly with questions.
The correct versions of your textbook have been ordered through the University bookstore.  There are both print versions as well as electronic versions available. Please keep in mind that there are multiple versions/editions of this book and there may be differences across versions/editions.  You will be responsible for mastering the material in a timely manner, and as contained in the editions carried by the bookstore.

Course Overview and How This Course Works
MGMT 3015: Introduction to Entrepreneurship (Fall 2026)
Instructor: Professor Shaker A. Zahra · zahra004@umn.edu (easiest way to reach me) · 612-702-1666 (cell)
Office hours: Monday/Wednesday 4:00–5:00 pm and by appointment, 3-390 Marilyn C. Nelson Hall

Teaching Assistant: Mr. Wayne Chou · chou0211@umn.edu · 4-169 Marilyn C. Nelson Hall · Office hours: TBD

Class meetings: Monday/Wednesday, 2:30–4:10 pm, Hanson Hall 1-111

E-mail format: Please include [MGMT 3015: 3] in the subject line of all e-mails and Canvas messages.

Teaching approach
This is a hands-on course designed to help you build a foundation of the knowledge, tools and techniques used by entrepreneurs. We will explore these topics through the textbook, case studies, team projects, small-group interaction with the instructor, and occasional guest speakers from the community. The fundamentals of entrepreneurship are best learned through action: engaging customers, testing ideas, and doing the hard work required to turn concepts into ventures.

Required textbook
Zacharakis, Bygrave & Corbett, Entrepreneurship, 5th ed. (Wiley, 2019; ISBN 978-1-119-56309-9) — available as an Inclusive Access eTextbook through Canvas and in print/electronic form at the University bookstore. Multiple editions exist; you are responsible for the edition carried by the bookstore.
Additional material (including short cases) will be posted in the Canvas modules.
Inclusive Access: watch for an e-mail with the subject "Important Course Materials Info: Charges to Your Student Account" (sent from no-reply@verbasoftware.com, so check your spam folder). It explains the program and how to opt out. Questions: inclusiveaccess@umn.edu.
Assignments and grading summary
Component    %    Due
Profile of a successful entrepreneur (individual, 2 pages)    5    9/21
New business idea (individual, 1 page)    5    9/25
Quiz 1 (individual)    10    10/7
Business idea pitches (group, 1 set of slides per group)    10    11/4 & 11/9
Reflection on idea pitches (individual, 2 pages)    5    11/11
Quiz 2 (individual)    10    12/2
Final presentation (group, 1 set of slides per group)    10    12/14 & 12/16
Written business plan (group, 1 plan per group)    25    12/16
Class participation (individual)    20    Entire semester
Total    100    
Detailed guidelines and grading rubrics for the larger assignments are posted with each assignment in the modules. Quizzes cover assigned readings and cases, course slides, and class discussions, and include short discussion, true/false and multiple-choice questions. If you must miss a quiz for a documented reason, e-mail the professor at least two weeks in advance to schedule a make-up.

Time commitment
Expect to spend 6–8 hours per week outside of class on average. Lighter weeks involve reading, scanning the news, networking and idea-journal entries; heavier weeks involve case or quiz preparation, venture planning, and presentation and business-plan preparation.

Class participation and expectations
This course consists of required in-person meetings. If you must miss a class, review the class recording and lecture slides on Canvas and coordinate with a classmate for notes.
Come on time, stay for the full session, and treat class like an important client meeting.
Connect your comments to concepts from the readings and the course. If speaking up in class is difficult for you, come to office hours to discuss strategies.
Use electronic devices only for class purposes; laptops and phones not needed for an activity should be muted and put away.
Use of ChatGPT and other AI tools
You are encouraged to use ChatGPT and similar tools, with three caveats: (1) clearly give credit for any material used; (2) you are responsible for the accuracy of the information; and (3) you may not use these tools to write your reports.

The complete syllabus, including all University policies, is available under Syllabus in the course navigation. The course will follow the projected schedule as closely as possible, but the schedule is subject to change, especially in the final weeks (presentations and speaker sessions). Changes will be announced in class and on Canvas.

# MGMT 3015 (001) — Introduction to Entrepreneurship
Fall'26, 4 credits. Entrepreneurship Minor core course. Per [[APAS]] its 4 credits also count against the 6 remaining general Elective Credits, so this one class serves two requirement buckets at once. Full syllabus and a separate "Course Overview and How This Course Works" Canvas page both read in full, pasted 2026-09-10.
## Source of Truth
`D:\Users\_Anant\10_Areas\UMN\Classes\Minor\MGMT 3015` is the real folder for this course, empty as of 2026-09-10 - both source documents came from pasted Canvas text, not downloaded PDFs. Desktop shortcut: `MGMT 3015.lnk` in `D:\Users\_Anant\Inbox\`.
## Catalog Info
Course number MGMT 3015, **Introduction to Entrepreneurship**, Section 001, Fall 2026, 4 credits. **September 8 - December 16, 2026**, Mondays and Wednesdays, **2:30-4:10pm, Hanson Hall 1-111.**
## Instructor & TA
**Professor Shaker A. Zahra** - Strategic Management & Entrepreneurship Dept., Carlson School of Management. zahra004@umn.edu (his own stated "easiest way to reach me"), (612) 702-1666 (cell), Office 3-390 Marilyn C. Nelson Hall. Office Hours: **M/W 4:00-5:00pm and by appointment.**
**TA: Mr. Wayne Chou**, PhD Candidate. chou0211@umn.edu, Office 4-169 Marilyn C. Nelson Hall. Office Hours: TBD.
> [!NOTE] Required email subject line
> All e-mails and Canvas messages must include **`[MGMT 3015: 3]`** in the subject line, per the instructor's own instructions - not a typo to "fix," this is the literal required tag.
## Course Goals
Four stated objectives: develop the capacity to generate, filter, and recognize the most feasible and profitable business ideas amid market noise; improve the ability to champion ideas in writing and face-to-face with stakeholders (investors/lenders, customers/community, suppliers/partners, current/future team members); determine the most effective business model and strategy for moving an idea from concept to reality; get a real taste of how founders actually build startups - from research and product/service design through founding-team formation to an initial sales push.
## Teaching Approach
Explicitly hands-on: "the fundamentals of entrepreneurship are best learned through action - engaging customers, testing ideas, and doing the hard work required to turn concepts into ventures." Delivered through the textbook, case studies, team projects, small-group interaction with the instructor, and occasional guest speakers from the community.
## Required Textbook
**Zacharakis, Bygrave & Corbett**, *Entrepreneurship*, **5th ed.** (Wiley, 2019; ISBN 978-1-119-56309-9), 512 pages. Available as an Inclusive Access eTextbook through Canvas, and in print/electronic form at the University bookstore.
> [!WARNING] Multiple editions exist - you're responsible for the bookstore's specific edition
> The syllabus states this directly: "there may be differences across versions/editions... you will be responsible for mastering the material... as contained in the editions carried by the bookstore." Don't buy a cheaper older edition assuming content is identical.
Additional material, including short cases, gets posted directly in the Canvas modules - not in the textbook.
## Inclusive Access Opt-Out
A separate email arrives with the subject "Important Course Materials Info: Charges to Your Student Account," from `no-reply@verbasoftware.com` (often lands in spam) - it explains the automatic textbook-charge program and how to opt out if buying the book independently. Questions go to `inclusiveaccess@umn.edu` directly.
## Grading
> [!WARNING] "Course assignments are not weighted" is a Canvas display artifact, not the real policy
> The syllabus page itself states "Course assignments are not weighted" - this is Canvas's own generic disclaimer that the instructor didn't use Canvas's native weighted-assignment-groups feature, not a claim that grades are unweighted in practice. The actual percentage breakdown below comes from the separate "Course Overview and How This Course Works" page, which explicitly sums to 100% - treat that table, not the syllabus page's boilerplate line, as the real grading structure.
| Component | Type | Weight | Due |
|---|---|---|---|
| Profile of a Successful Entrepreneur | Individual, 2 pages | 5% | 9/21 |
| New Business Idea | Individual, 1 page | 5% | 9/25 |
| Quiz 1 | Individual | 10% | 10/7 |
| Business Idea Pitches | Group, 1 slide set per group | 10% | 11/4 & 11/9 |
| Reflection on Idea Pitches | Individual, 2 pages | 5% | 11/11 |
| Quiz 2 | Individual | 10% | 12/2 |
| Final Presentation | Group, 1 slide set per group | 10% | 12/14 & 12/16 |
| Written Business Plan | Group, 1 plan per group | 25% | 12/16 |
| Class Participation | Individual | 20% | Entire semester |
| **Total** | | **100%** | |
Detailed guidelines and rubrics for the larger assignments get posted alongside each assignment in the Canvas modules, not in the syllabus itself.
## Quizzes
Cover assigned readings and cases, course slides, and class discussions - format is short discussion questions plus true/false and multiple-choice. **Missing a quiz for a documented reason requires emailing the professor at least two weeks in advance** to schedule a make-up - this is not a same-week accommodation.
## Time Commitment
**6-8 hours/week outside class**, averaged - lighter weeks: reading, scanning relevant news, networking, idea-journal entries; heavier weeks: case or quiz preparation, venture planning, and presentation/business-plan preparation. Against [[10_Areas/Life/Plans/Fall 2026/Fall 2026 Plan|Fall 2026 Plan]]'s 80/20 split, this is a mid-weight Fall'26 course - heavier than [[20_Progress/Degree/ENGL 1004/ENGL 1004 Board|ENGL 1004]]'s ~8.5 hrs but lighter than [[20_Progress/Degree/CSCI 4061/CSCI 4061 Board|CSCI 4061]]'s ~12.
## Class Participation & Expectations
In-person attendance is required by design, not merely encouraged. If a class must be missed: review the recording and lecture slides on Canvas, and coordinate with a classmate for notes - there's no separate excused-absence process described beyond this. Explicit behavioral expectations: come on time, stay the full session, "treat class like an important client meeting"; connect comments to actual reading/course concepts rather than general opinion; if speaking up in class is difficult, come to office hours to discuss strategies rather than staying silent all semester. Electronic devices are for class purposes only - anything not needed for the current activity gets muted and put away.
## AI Policy
> [!TIP] The most permissive AI policy of any Fall'26 course
> "You are encouraged to use ChatGPT and similar tools," with three explicit caveats: (1) clearly credit any AI-generated material used; (2) you are personally responsible for the accuracy of whatever the tool produces; (3) **you may not use these tools to write your reports.** This is a real contrast with [[20_Progress/Degree/ENGL 1004/ENGL 1004 Board|ENGL 1004]]'s total ban and [[20_Progress/Degree/CSCI 4061/CSCI 4061 Board|CSCI 4061]]'s near-total one - here, AI is a sanctioned research/ideation tool, and the line is drawn only at the final-writing step, not at using AI at all.
## Schedule
Only a two-row fragment of the full session-by-session schedule was captured, covering the very end of the semester - the rest (Sept 8 through early December) hasn't been pulled yet.
| Row | Date | Session | Due |
|---|---|---|---|
| 27 | (date not captured) | Final Presentation, Group B - all members present | — |
| 28 | Wed 12/16 | Final Presentation, Group A - all members present | All written business plans and slides |
This confirms the grading table's "Final Presentation due 12/14 & 12/16" split: Group B presents first (likely Mon 12/14, though that exact date wasn't in the captured fragment - don't treat it as confirmed), Group A presents Wed 12/16, and every group's written business plan and slide deck is due by that same Wednesday regardless of which day that group actually presented.

# MGMT 3015 (001) — Preparation
==Group deliverables (Business Idea Pitches, Final Presentation, Written Business Plan) are 45% of the grade combined, and Class Participation alone is 20% - together that's 65% riding on team quality and consistent live engagement, not on any single written assignment.==
## Where the Points Actually Are
Written Business Plan (25%) is the single largest component, followed by Class Participation (20%) - together 45% from just two lines. Add the two group presentations (Business Idea Pitches 10%, Final Presentation 10%) and group/team-dependent work is **45% of the total grade**, with individual writing (Profile 5%, New Business Idea 5%, Reflection 5%) and the two Quizzes (10% each = 20%) making up the rest. Picking a functional team early matters more here than in most Fall'26 courses - the Written Business Plan alone is worth as much as both quizzes combined.
## Cheapest Points
Class Participation (20%) has no separate exam or rubric-graded deliverable behind it - it's earned through the behaviors [[MGMT 3015 Board]] states directly (on time, full session, comments tied to real concepts). This is real, sustained effort but not a skill-execution risk the way a written plan or a graded quiz is - showing up prepared every session captures a fifth of the grade with no single point of failure.
## The Real Traps
- **"Course assignments are not weighted" on the syllabus page is a Canvas artifact, not real policy** - the actual grading table (in the separate Course Overview page) sums cleanly to 100% and is the one to plan against. See [[MGMT 3015 Board]]'s Grading warning.
- **Quiz make-ups need two weeks' notice, not a same-week email** - this is stricter than it sounds; a documented reason discovered the week of the quiz is already too late to guarantee a make-up slot.
- **Required email subject tag `[MGMT 3015: 3]`** - easy to forget and functionally invisible until a real email needs a fast reply and gets buried or delayed for missing it.
- **The Written Business Plan and Final Presentation share the same 12/16 deadline** regardless of which day your group actually presents (Group A or Group B) - per [[MGMT 3015 Board]]'s Schedule fragment, presenting earlier (12/14) doesn't buy any extra time on the plan itself.
- **The specific textbook edition matters** - "5th ed." is stated explicitly; multiple editions exist and the syllabus puts the burden on the student to master the bookstore's specific edition, not whichever is cheapest online.
## Study Cadence
6-8 hours/week outside class, unevenly distributed per [[MGMT 3015 Board]]'s Time Commitment section - lighter weeks (reading, news-scanning, networking, idea-journal entries) alternate with heavier weeks (case/quiz prep, venture planning, presentation/plan work). The two Quizzes (10/7 and 12/2) cover readings, cases, slides, *and* class discussion - meaning quiz prep can't be done from the textbook alone, live class attendance is load-bearing for exam content in a way it isn't in a pure-lecture course.
## Open Question

# MGMT 3015 — Textbook Map
==Every one of the textbook's 13 chapters gets a reading assignment somewhere in the semester - full coverage, no chapter skipped - but the course teaches them wildly out of numerical order, and one thematic session (Internationalization) uses slides instead of a chapter at all.== Cross-referenced against the full Schedule table in [[MGMT 3015 Board]], pasted from Canvas Modules 2026-09-09.
## Zacharakis, Bygrave & Corbett — Entrepreneurship (5th ed., Wiley 2019)
In the order the course actually teaches them, not numerical order:
- **Ch 1 & 2** - Session 2, Mon 9/14: What Does It Take to Be an Entrepreneur.
- **Ch 2** (continued) - Session 3, Wed 9/16: Entrepreneurial Personality.
- **Ch 3 & 4** - Session 4, Mon 9/21: Creativity and Idea Generation.
- **Ch 3 & 4** (continued) - Session 5, Wed 9/23: Opportunities - Definition & Evaluation.
- **Ch 8** - Session 8, Mon 10/5, and Session 9, Wed 10/7: The Business Plan. Session 9 also carries Quiz 1.
- **Ch 6** - Session 10, Mon 10/12: Industry Analysis.
- **Ch 6** (continued) - Session 12, Mon 10/19: Strategy.
- **Ch 5** - Session 13, Wed 10/21: Business Models.
- **Ch 12** - Session 15, Wed 10/28, and Session 16, *10/31 (dated a Saturday - see [[MGMT 3015 Board]]'s Schedule warning, likely means Mon 11/2)*: Marketing, parts 1 and 2.
- **Ch 9, 10 & 11** - Session 19, Wed 11/11, and Session 20, Mon 11/16: Financing, parts 1 and 2.
- **Ch 7** - Session 23, Mon 11/30: The Venture Team.
- **Ch 13** - Session 25, Mon 12/7: Growth.
None of these have an individual chapter note yet - each gets created once its session is actually covered in lecture, per [[Course Week Standard]].
## The One Session With No Chapter: Internationalization
Session 22 (Mon 11/23) covers Internationalization using slides only ("to be posted here" per the Canvas module) - no chapter of the textbook is assigned for it. This is the one gap in an otherwise complete 1-13 chapter mapping; don't create a chapter note for it, since there's no chapter to map it to.
## Why the Out-of-Order Sequence Is Worth Noting
The Business Plan (Ch 8) is taught in Module 4, *before* Industry Analysis, Strategy, and Business Models (Ch 6, 6, 5 in Module 5) - meaning students draft plan mechanics before covering some of the analytical content that would normally inform a plan's content. This isn't a data error; it's confirmed by two independent module titles ("Module 4: The Business Plan & Quiz 1" then "Module 5: Industry Analysis, Strategy & Business Models") and matches the course's own stated hands-on philosophy in [[MGMT 3015 Board]] - start building the plan early, layer in the analytical tools as the semester continues, rather than front-loading all theory before any real drafting starts.
## Standard
Each chapter note, once created, follows [[Textbook Template]] - one highlight anchor, bolded key concepts, a worked example, a connection back to the matching session, and flashcards.
## Status
Zero chapter notes written as of 2026-09-09 - the course has only reached Session 1 (Wed 9/9, pure introduction, no assigned chapter). This map plans the full chapter-to-session structure before any individual chapter note exists, instead of inventing it piecemeal as the semester goes.

# CSCI 4521 — Applied Machine Learning
Fall'26, 3 credits. CSCI-designated Technical Elective. Per [[APAS]] this is the class that closes Upper-Division Major Credits exactly at 19/19 - nothing else in the current plan does that on its own. Syllabus read in full from the source folder, 2026-09-10.
## Source of Truth
`D:\Users\_Anant\10_Areas\UMN\Classes\CSCI\CSCI 4521` is the real folder for this course - it already holds `Textbook & Resources/` (the syllabus PDF plus large local copies of the two optional reference books) and an empty `Lectures/` folder waiting for slides. Desktop shortcut: `CSCI 4521.lnk` in `D:\Users\_Anant\Inbox\`.
> [!DANGER] The source syllabus PDF is dated "Fall 2025" - not confirmed current for this semester
> `Syllabus - CSCI 4521.pdf` states "Fall 2025" at the top, with instructor Bernardo Bianco Prado, meeting time Tue/Thu 2:30-3:45pm in Amundson Hall B75, and a named TA roster. None of this has been cross-checked against an actual Fall'26 Canvas page - it may be an intentionally-reused syllabus template (grading structure and topics carried forward unchanged year to year, which is common) or it may be genuinely last year's document. **Confirm the instructor, meeting time/location, and TA names on Canvas before trusting any of them** - the policies, grading breakdown, and topic list are lower-risk to reuse since that content tends to be stable across offerings, but still unconfirmed for this exact semester.
## Catalog Info (As Captured - Fall 2025 Label, Unconfirmed for Fall'26)
**Bernardo Bianco Prado** (he/him) - bianc072@umn.edu - Office Lind Hall 300H (office hours or by appointment only) - Office Hours **MWTh 1:00-2:00pm**. Meeting time as printed: **Tue/Thu 2:30-3:45pm, Amundson Hall B75.**
*Graduate TAs:* Ebasa Temesgen (temes021@umn.edu), Zhongxing Zhang (zhan8889@umn.edu).
*Undergraduate TAs:* Eddie Jang (zhan8168@umn.edu - note: this TA's listed email uses the "zhan" prefix, identical in form to Zhongxing Zhang's - possibly a copy-paste error in the source document, not something to silently correct), John Palmer (palm0545@umn.edu), Harini Kuchibhotla (kuchi027@umn.edu), Alistair White (whit3491@umn.edu), Ethan Phua (phua0003@umn.edu).
## Course Objective
An introduction to solving problems with machine learning: classifying data, supervised vs. unsupervised learning, numerical optimization, linear/non-linear regression, neural networks/deep learning - plus the practical surrounding skills (loading/cleaning datasets, visualization, model interpretation, ethics in ML). Mix of in-class discussion, hands-on coding, projects, and quizzes. Explicitly "Applied" - uses modern frameworks (PyTorch) to handle the underlying math, contrasted against CSCI 5521/5525 (which build the algorithmic/mathematical foundations directly) and CSCI 5523/5527/5541/5561 (which go deep on one specialized application area this course only introduces).
## Course Materials
**Primary, free online text:** *An Introduction to Statistical Learning, with Applications in Python* (James, Witten, Hastie, Tibshirani, Taylor) - a local copy exists at `Textbook & Resources/Primary Textbook CSCI 4521.pdf`.
**Optional, not required:** *Machine Learning System Design Interview* (Aminian & Xu) - useful for industry interview prep, no local copy; *Deep Learning Book* (Goodfellow, Bengio, Courville), free online, local copy at `Textbook & Resources/Secondary Textbook.pdf`. Neither optional book is required.
Piazza is the class discussion platform, monitored daily by staff - collaboration on questions is encouraged, but any post containing an actual homework/quiz answer gets deleted.
## Prerequisites
Comfortable Python (functions, packages, debugging) is the most important one. Also expected: intro calculus (slopes/derivatives), basic probability/stats (probabilities, multivariate Gaussians, standard deviations), basic linear algebra (vectors, basis, vector spaces). Pandas and Matplotlib get a brief in-class introduction early on, but you're expected to have the "software development maturity" to self-teach the rest from documentation.
## Class Participation & In-Class Materials
Questions are explicitly expected, including on very recent work the instructor may not have clean answers for. In-class activities (chime-in responses) are graded on completion/demonstrated effort, done in-class only, cannot be made up - **lowest 3 dropped**. Bring pen/pencil and paper daily; bring a laptop when activities involve coding (phone/tablet or teaming with a neighbor are stated fallbacks if no laptop is available).
## Programming Language & Tools
Python throughout - "the de facto accepted standard for ML." Libraries used across the semester: Jupyter notebooks, Pandas, Matplotlib, Seaborn, Scikit-Learn, PyTorch.
## Grading (Approximate, Per the Syllabus's Own Label)
| Component | Weight |
|---|---|
| Homework 1-4 | 40% |
| Quizzes 1-6 | 30% |
| Final Project | 20% |
| Participation Activities | 10% |
The syllabus itself calls this breakdown "approximate," unlike every other Fall'26 course's grading table - worth remembering if the real weights shift slightly once graded.
## Homework
4 assignments total, each a combination of a well-documented Jupyter notebook (Python code) and a short written report (PDF). Regrade requests go to the TA who originally graded, escalate to named graduate TAs then the instructor if needed, and must be submitted **within 7 calendar days** of the grade being released.
## Quizzes
6 total, **biweekly, in class on Thursdays**, taken at the end of class. Focused on interpreting method results on new datasets - explicitly framed as similar to technical-interview or research-presentation questions, not textbook recall. **Two pages of notes allowed** (front and back, printed or handwritten, must be on paper).
## Final Project
Student-led: a short proposal, a presentation, and the coding work - **counts approximately 2x a single homework** in effort/weight.
## Late Policy
> [!TIP] The most forgiving late policy of any Fall'26 course
> Every assignment gets an automatic **24-hour grace period with zero penalty** - not a reduced-credit late window like [[20_Progress/Degree/CSCI 4061/CSCI 4061 Board|CSCI 4061]]'s tiered penalties, a full no-penalty grace day. Nothing is accepted after that 24-hour window closes, though - it's forgiving, not unlimited.
For a genuine special/life circumstance beyond the grace day, the instructor must be contacted **within 72 hours** of being able to do so (the syllabus's own example: losing a laptop means emailing within 72 hours of realizing it's lost, not 72 hours from the original deadline).
## Extra Credit
Some assignments/activities offer small extra-credit opportunities, aimed at students who want to go deeper on a topic they're personally interested in - not a fixed, guaranteed category.
## AI Policy
> [!TIP] A three-tier, per-assignment AI policy - not one blanket rule
> Every assignment gets an explicit color-coded AI designation: 🟢 **encouraged** (develop your AI skills), 🟡 **technically allowed but discouraged** (try it yourself first), or 🔴 **banned** (goes against that assignment's learning objectives). This is a genuinely different shape than any other Fall'26 course's AI policy - check each assignment's own color before assuming a blanket rule applies. Additional real caveats: you're fully responsible for verifying anything an AI tool outputs (they're not fact-checked or peer-reviewed); data security varies by tool - UMN provides a Copilot instance with enterprise data protection, but plain ChatGPT/ungated tools may retain whatever you paste in as future training data, which the syllabus explicitly calls out as an academic-integrity risk if course materials or others' copyrighted work gets uploaded to an unprotected model.
## Topics (5 Units, No Calendar Dates Given)
> [!WARNING] No session-by-session schedule exists in this syllabus
> Unlike every other Fall'26 course's Board note, there is no date-anchored weekly calendar here - only these five thematic units in teaching order, with no dates attached. A real week-by-week schedule needs to come from Canvas Modules directly, the same way it did for [[20_Progress/Degree/MGMT 3015/MGMT 3015 Board|MGMT 3015]] and [[20_Progress/Degree/CSCI 4511W/CSCI 4511W Board|CSCI 4511W]].
1. **ML Overview** - problem types (supervised/unsupervised, regression/classification), core tools (NumPy, Pandas, Scikit-Learn); topics include CSV/tabular data, KNN, classification metrics.
2. **Unsupervised Learning** - finding structure in observed data (e.g., clustering customers by purchase pattern); topics include text data, K-Means, PCA, Gaussian Mixture Models.
3. **Regression** - fitting models to predict/interpolate/extrapolate; topics include error/residual measurement, numerical optimization with PyTorch.
4. **Classification** - assigning data to categories; topics include image data, Logistic Regression, ROC/AUC, model selection.
5. **Neural Networks / Deep Learning** - non-linear models for complex, large-scale data; topics include Multi-Layer Perceptrons, loss functions (Cross Entropy, L1/L2), Convolutional Networks, and RNNs "if time permits."
Every unit also touches ethics in ML, visualizing results, and ML in the news - and, depending on pacing/interest, may extend into generative AI models, pre-trained models, zero-shot learning, and LLMs.

# CSCI 4521 — Textbook Map
==Unlike every other Fall'26 course, this one has no purchase-required textbook at all - the primary text is free online, and neither book named in the syllabus is technically required.== Cross-referenced against the Topics section in [[CSCI 4521 Board]] - no chapter-to-date mapping exists yet since the syllabus itself gives no calendar.
## Primary — An Introduction to Statistical Learning, with Applications in Python (James, Witten, Hastie, Tibshirani, Taylor)
Free online, the course's actual working text despite not being labeled "required." A local copy sits at `Textbook & Resources/Primary Textbook CSCI 4521.pdf` in the source folder (confirmed present, 2026-09-10, ~20MB - not opened in full here). No chapter-by-topic mapping exists yet because [[CSCI 4521 Board]]'s Topics section lists 5 thematic units with no chapter numbers or dates attached - this gets filled in once real lecture dates and reading assignments appear on Canvas, the same way [[20_Progress/Degree/CSCI 4511W/Textbook/Textbook Map|CSCI 4511W's]] map was built from its Canvas Modules page rather than its syllabus alone.
Plausible unit-to-chapter alignment based on ISLP's own well-known structure (standard editions cover: intro/statistical learning basics, linear regression, classification, resampling methods, tree-based methods, support vector machines, unsupervised learning, deep learning) - **this is an inference from the book's general structure, not a confirmed course mapping**, and shouldn't be treated as one until Canvas names real chapters:
- Unit 1 (ML Overview) - likely ISLP's introductory/statistical-learning-foundations chapters.
- Unit 2 (Unsupervised Learning) - likely ISLP's unsupervised learning chapter (clustering, PCA).
- Unit 3 (Regression) - likely ISLP's linear regression chapter.
- Unit 4 (Classification) - likely ISLP's classification chapter.
- Unit 5 (Neural Networks/Deep Learning) - likely ISLP's deep learning chapter (a newer addition to the Python edition specifically).
## Optional — Deep Learning Book (Goodfellow, Bengio, Courville)
Free online, not required. A local copy sits at `Textbook & Resources/Secondary Textbook.pdf` (~12MB, not opened in full here). Syllabus frames this as useful "especially if you intend to use machine learning in an industry position someday" - a depth reference for Unit 5 specifically, not assigned reading.

*Machine Learning System Design Interview* (Aminian & Xu) - no local copy exists, not required, named purely as an interview-prep resource rather than course material. Doesn't belong in a chapter-mapping structure at all; noted here only so it isn't confused with an actual assigned reading later.

# CSCI 4521 — Preparation
==Homework (40%) and Quizzes (30%) are 70% of the grade, and the lowest 3 participation activities get dropped automatically - the real risk here isn't a single bad assignment, it's falling behind on the hands-on coding habit the whole course is built around.==
## Where the Points Actually Are
Homework 1-4 (40%) and the Final Project (20%, ~2x a single homework in effort) together are 60% of the grade in coding-and-report form - Quizzes (30%) and Participation (10%) make up the rest. Unlike most other Fall'26 courses, there's no midterm/final exam at all - everything is homework, quizzes, project, and participation.
## Cheapest Points
Participation activities (10%) have a built-in cushion - **lowest 3 dropped** automatically, and they're graded on completion/effort, not correctness. Quizzes allow two pages of notes (front and back), which softens the memorization burden compared to a closed-book exam.
## The Real Traps
- **The syllabus is dated "Fall 2025"** - instructor, meeting time, and TA roster are all unconfirmed for the actual Fall'26 offering. Confirm on Canvas before trusting any of it, per [[CSCI 4521 Board]]'s warning.
- **No date-anchored schedule exists anywhere in the source syllabus** - only 5 topic units with no calendar. Real weekly planning here depends on a Canvas Modules pull that hasn't happened yet, the same way [[20_Progress/Degree/MGMT 3015/MGMT 3015 Board|MGMT 3015]]'s and [[20_Progress/Degree/CSCI 4511W/CSCI 4511W Board|CSCI 4511W]]'s real schedules only became known once their Modules pages were captured.
- **Per-assignment AI policy, not a blanket rule** - every assignment gets its own green/yellow/red designation. Using AI on a red-flagged assignment isn't a gray area the way it might be in a course with one policy for everything.
- **In-class activities cannot be made up under any circumstance** - unlike the homework/quiz late-work grace day below, missing a chime-in activity in person loses those points permanently (unless it falls in the dropped-lowest-3).
- **The instructor lectures live by writing code in Google Colab** - per direct observation, the actual teaching format is the professor writing code himself in real time, with the class following along and annotating with comments, rather than pre-built slides carrying the lecture. Prior textbook/resource reading before class matters more here than in a slide-driven course, since class time itself is spent extending that base knowledge live rather than introducing it from scratch - the useful question to ask in class is "what exactly does this do," not "what is this concept," since the concept should already be familiar going in.
## Study Cadence
> [!TIP] The most forgiving late policy of any Fall'26 course
> A full 24-hour, zero-penalty grace day on every assignment, no exceptions process needed - just don't need more than a day. Genuine life circumstances beyond that need instructor contact within 72 hours of being able to do so.
Given the live-coding lecture format, the highest-leverage habit is reading the relevant section of *An Introduction to Statistical Learning* (see [[20_Progress/Degree/CSCI 4521/Textbook/Textbook Map|Textbook Map]]) before each class covering a new topic, not after - lecture time is spent building on that base in real time via Colab, not re-explaining it from zero. Quizzes are biweekly on Thursdays and focus on interpreting results on new datasets rather than recalling definitions, so practice applying each unit's methods to an unfamiliar dataset before its quiz, not just reviewing notes.
## Weekly Study Plan
> [!WARNING] No calendar exists in the source syllabus - this is organized by topic unit, not by week
> Estimated at **~9 hours/week total** via the standard 3 hrs/credit convention (3 credits) - not a stated figure. Of that, ~2.5 hrs is the two weekly lectures (Tue/Thu, time unconfirmed for Fall'26). The table below is the 5 topic units in teaching order, per [[CSCI 4521 Board]]'s Topics section - once real Canvas Modules dates exist, this should be rebuilt into an actual weekly table the way [[20_Progress/Degree/MGMT 3015/Preparation|MGMT 3015's]] was.
| Unit | Topic | Key Methods | Likely Textbook Area |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | ML Overview | KNN, classification metrics, NumPy/Pandas/Scikit-Learn basics | ISLP intro / foundations chapters (unconfirmed mapping) |
| 2 | Unsupervised Learning | K-Means, PCA, Gaussian Mixture Models | ISLP unsupervised learning chapter (unconfirmed) |
| 3 | Regression | Error/residual measurement, numerical optimization with PyTorch | ISLP linear regression chapter (unconfirmed) |
| 4 | Classification | Logistic Regression, ROC/AUC, model selection | ISLP classification chapter (unconfirmed) |
| 5 | Neural Networks / Deep Learning | MLP, loss functions, CNNs, maybe RNNs | ISLP deep learning chapter (unconfirmed) |
Every unit also touches ethics in ML, visualization, and ML in the news, per the Board note - not itemized per-unit here since the syllabus doesn't break that out either.

\
# -*- coding: utf-8 -*-
"""
Build the Fall'26 Semester Calendar.xlsx from verified session data.
Every event is (course, date, code) or (course, date, topic_fragment) - weekday and
week-block are computed from the real date, never hand-guessed, to avoid placement errors.
"""
import datetime as dt
from openpyxl import Workbook
from openpyxl.styles import Font, Alignment, PatternFill, Border, Side
from openpyxl.utils import get_column_letter

D = dt.date

COURSES = ["ENGL 1004", "CSCI 4511W", "CSCI 4061", "CSCI 5304", "MGMT 3015", "CSCI 4521"]

# 15 weeks + finals week. (week_num_label, monday, sunday, note)
WEEKS = [
    ("Week 1", D(2026,9,7), D(2026,9,11), "Labor Day Mon 9/7 - classes start Tue 9/8"),
    ("Week 2", D(2026,9,14), D(2026,9,18), ""),
    ("Week 3", D(2026,9,21), D(2026,9,25), ""),
    ("Week 4", D(2026,9,28), D(2026,10,2), ""),
    ("Week 5", D(2026,10,5), D(2026,10,9), ""),
    ("Week 6", D(2026,10,12), D(2026,10,16), ""),
    ("Week 7", D(2026,10,19), D(2026,10,23), ""),
    ("Week 8", D(2026,10,26), D(2026,10,30), ""),
    ("Week 9", D(2026,11,2), D(2026,11,6), ""),
    ("Week 10", D(2026,11,9), D(2026,11,13), ""),
    ("Week 11", D(2026,11,16), D(2026,11,20), ""),
    ("Week 12", D(2026,11,23), D(2026,11,27), "Thanksgiving Thu 11/26 - Fri 11/27, no class"),
    ("Week 13", D(2026,11,30), D(2026,12,4), ""),
    ("Week 14", D(2026,12,7), D(2026,12,11), ""),
    ("Week 15", D(2026,12,14), D(2026,12,16), "last day of instruction Wed 12/16"),
    ("Finals Week", D(2026,12,17), D(2026,12,23), ""),
]
DAYNAMES = ["Monday","Tuesday","Wednesday","Thursday","Friday","Saturday","Sunday"]

def week_index_for(date):
    for i,(label, mon, sun, note) in enumerate(WEEKS):
        if mon <= date <= sun:
            return i
    return None

# events[course] = list of (date, code_string)
events = {c: [] for c in COURSES}
# topics[course][week_index] = list of short summary fragments
topics = {c: {i: [] for i in range(len(WEEKS))} for c in COURSES}

def ev(course, date, code):
    events[course].append((date, code))

def top(course, week_idx, text):
    topics[course][week_idx].append(text)

# ============================================================ ENGL 1004 ============================================================
E = "ENGL 1004"
ev(E, D(2026,9,8), "lec: Intro")
ev(E, D(2026,9,10), "lec: case law/state laws")
ev(E, D(2026,9,15), "lec: state laws | hw: HF ch1-6, Journal1 due")
ev(E, D(2026,9,17), "lec: Camp of Saints 1-3,6,11[Canvas]")
ev(E, D(2026,9,22), "lec: Camp of Saints 13,19; Island Trees v Pico | Journal2 due")
ev(E, D(2026,9,24), "lec: Island Trees cont | hw: read Melissa, Journal3 due")
ev(E, D(2026,9,29), "lec: role planning | hw: read Bluest Eye")
ev(E, D(2026,10,1), "lec: Bluest Eye/Tango, sim planning | role sketch due")
ev(E, D(2026,10,6), "lec: Bluest Eye/Melissa, sim planning")
ev(E, D(2026,10,8), "SIMULATION day 1")
ev(E, D(2026,10,13), "SIMULATION day 2 | Journal4 due")
ev(E, D(2026,10,15), "reading day")
ev(E, D(2026,10,20), "SIMULATION day 3 | Journal5 due")
ev(E, D(2026,10,22), "sim debrief | Journal6 due")
ev(E, D(2026,10,27), "lec: Handmaid's Tale")
ev(E, D(2026,10,29), "lec: Handmaid's Tale | Journal7 due")
ev(E, D(2026,11,3), "lec: Handmaid's Tale")
ev(E, D(2026,11,5), "lec: Huck Finn | Journal8 due")
ev(E, D(2026,11,10), "lec: Huck Finn")
ev(E, D(2026,11,12), "lec: Huck Finn | Journal9 due")
ev(E, D(2026,11,17), "lec: Drama | Journal10 due")
ev(E, D(2026,11,19), "lec: Maus I")
ev(E, D(2026,11,24), "lec: Maus I&II | Journal11 due")
ev(E, D(2026,11,26), "NO CLASS (Thanksgiving)")
ev(E, D(2026,12,1), "lec: Fun House | Journal12 due")
ev(E, D(2026,12,3), "lec: Fun House")
ev(E, D(2026,12,8), "lec: Bluest Eye (revisit)")
ev(E, D(2026,12,10), "exam review")
ev(E, D(2026,12,15), "EXAM: Final, in class")

# ============================================================ MGMT 3015 ============================================================
M = "MGMT 3015"
ev(M, D(2026,9,9), "lec: Course Overview/Intro")
ev(M, D(2026,9,14), "lec: tb Ch1&2 - What It Takes")
ev(M, D(2026,9,16), "lec: tb Ch2 - Entrepreneurial Personality")
ev(M, D(2026,9,21), "lec: tb Ch3&4 - Creativity/Idea Gen | hw: Profile due (5%)")
ev(M, D(2026,9,23), "lec: tb Ch3&4 - Opportunities")
ev(M, D(2026,9,25), "[date anomaly=Fri not M/W] small groups | hw: New Biz Idea due (5%)")
ev(M, D(2026,9,30), "present ideas to class")
ev(M, D(2026,10,5), "lec: tb Ch8 - Business Plan | form groups 3-4")
ev(M, D(2026,10,7), "lec: tb Ch8 cont | qz: Quiz1 (10%)")
ev(M, D(2026,10,12), "lec: tb Ch6 - Industry Analysis")
ev(M, D(2026,10,14), "group applications work")
ev(M, D(2026,10,19), "lec: tb Ch6 - Strategy")
ev(M, D(2026,10,21), "lec: tb Ch5 - Business Models")
ev(M, D(2026,10,26), "in-class group work: idea pitch")
ev(M, D(2026,10,28), "lec: tb Ch12 - Marketing #1")
ev(M, D(2026,10,31), "[date anomaly=Sat, likely 11/2] lec: tb Ch12 - Marketing #2")
ev(M, D(2026,11,4), "Group Idea Pitches - Group A | hw: Pitch Slides due (10%)")
ev(M, D(2026,11,9), "Group Idea Pitches - Group B")
ev(M, D(2026,11,11), "lec: tb Ch9,10,11 - Financing 1 | hw: Reflection due (5%)")
ev(M, D(2026,11,16), "lec: tb Ch9,10,11 - Financing 2")
ev(M, D(2026,11,18), "group work: financing decisions")
ev(M, D(2026,11,23), "Internationalization (slides only)")
ev(M, D(2026,11,25), "NO CLASS (Thanksgiving)")
ev(M, D(2026,11,30), "lec: tb Ch7 - The Venture Team")
ev(M, D(2026,12,2), "qz: Quiz2 (10%) | work on biz plan")

for c in COURSES:

import json
with open("events_dump.json","w") as f:
    out = {c: [(str(d),code) for d,code in sorted(events[c])] for c in COURSES}
    json.dump(out, f, indent=1, default=str)
print("Dumped to events_dump.json for review")

# ============================================================ ORGANIZE INTO GRID ============================================================
# grid[course][week_idx][day_name] = list of code strings
grid = {c: {i: {d: [] for d in DAYNAMES} for i in range(len(WEEKS))} for c in COURSES}
for c in COURSES:
    for date, code in events[c]:
        wi = week_index_for(date)
        if wi is None:
            print(f"WARNING: {c} event on {date} does not fall in any defined week - skipped: {code}")
            continue
        day_name = DAYNAMES[date.weekday()]
        grid[c][wi][day_name].append(code)

def derive_topic(course, week_idx):
    """Pull a short 'what was covered' phrase from that week's lecture/reading events only."""
    frags = []
    for day in DAYNAMES:
        for code in grid[course][week_idx][day]:
            if code.startswith("lec:") or code.startswith("tb") or "tb(" in code or code.startswith("TBD") or code.startswith("NO CLASS") or code.startswith("SIMULATION") or code.startswith("EXAM"):
                # strip admin-only fragments, keep the substantive part before any " | "
                main = code.split(" | ")[0]
                frags.append(main.replace("lec: ", ""))
    # de-duplicate while preserving order
    seen = []
    for f in frags:
        if f not in seen:
            seen.append(f)
    return "; ".join(seen) if seen else ""

# ============================================================ WRITE WORKBOOK ============================================================
wb = Workbook()
ws = wb.active
ws.title = "Sheet1"

header_fill = PatternFill("solid", fgColor="1F4E78")
header_font = Font(bold=True, color="FFFFFF", size=10)
week_label_font = Font(bold=True, size=10)
course_font = Font(bold=True, size=9)
cell_font = Font(size=8)
topic_font = Font(size=8, italic=True)
thin = Side(style="thin", color="BFBFBF")
border = Border(left=thin, right=thin, top=thin, bottom=thin)
wrap = Alignment(wrap_text=True, vertical="top", horizontal="left")
center = Alignment(wrap_text=True, vertical="center", horizontal="center")
anomaly_fill = PatternFill("solid", fgColor="FFF2CC")
noclass_fill = PatternFill("solid", fgColor="F2F2F2")

HEADER = ["Week","Classes"] + DAYNAMES + ["Topics"]
col_widths = [26, 12, 20, 20, 20, 20, 20, 12, 12, 34]
for i,w in enumerate(col_widths, start=1):
    ws.column_dimensions[get_column_letter(i)].width = w

r = 1
for wi, (label, mon, sun, note) in enumerate(WEEKS):
    # header row for this week block
    for ci, h in enumerate(HEADER, start=1):
        cell = ws.cell(row=r, column=ci, value=h)
        cell.font = header_font
        cell.fill = header_fill
        cell.alignment = center
        cell.border = border
    header_row = r
    r += 1
    first_course_row = r
    date_str = f"{mon.strftime('%-m/%-d')} to {sun.strftime('%-m/%-d')}"
    if note:
        date_str += f" ({note})"
    for course in COURSES:
        ws.cell(row=r, column=1, value=None)  # filled after merge below
        cc = ws.cell(row=r, column=2, value=course)
        cc.font = course_font
        cc.border = border
        cc.alignment = wrap
        for di, day in enumerate(DAYNAMES, start=3):
            items = grid[course][wi][day]
            cell = ws.cell(row=r, column=di, value="\n".join(items) if items else None)
            cell.font = cell_font
            cell.alignment = wrap
            cell.border = border
            joined = " ".join(items)
            if "anomaly" in joined:
                cell.fill = anomaly_fill
            elif "NO CLASS" in joined or "Thanksgiving" in joined:
                cell.fill = noclass_fill
        topic_text = derive_topic(course, wi)
        tcell = ws.cell(row=r, column=10, value=topic_text if topic_text else None)
        tcell.font = topic_font
        tcell.alignment = wrap
        tcell.border = border
        r += 1
    last_course_row = r - 1
    # merge the week-label/date cell down the course rows, week label on top line
    wcell = ws.cell(row=first_course_row, column=1, value=f"{label}\n{date_str}")
    wcell.font = week_label_font
    wcell.alignment = wrap
    wcell.border = border
    ws.merge_cells(start_row=first_course_row, start_column=1, end_row=last_course_row, end_column=1)
    ws.row_dimensions[header_row].height = 16

ws.freeze_panes = "A2"
out_path = "D:/Users/_Anant/10_Areas/UMN/Plan/In Semester Review/Fall'26 Semester Calendar.xlsx"
wb.save(out_path)
print(f"Saved: {out_path}")
print(f"Total rows written: {r-1}")
